#6040. Trump highlights NASA nuclear-powered Mars spacecraft mission planned for 2028

President Donald Trump outlined ambitious plans for NASA to launch a nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars by 2028, return American astronauts to the moon and establish a permanent presence on the lunar surface.

President Donald Trump highlighted NASA's ambitious plans to launch its first nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars within the next two years, reaffirming his administration's push to expand American space exploration and eventually send astronauts to the red planet.

Speaking at the UP Summit at Kennedy Space Center in Cape Canaveral, Florida, Trump touted the mission as part of his administration's broader ambitions to expand America's presence beyond Earth.

"Within the next two years, NASA will launch the first-ever nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars," Trump said.

The president described the mission as a step toward eventually sending American astronauts to the planet.

TRUMP CALLS ISS CREW FROM HOUSTON TO PRAISE SPACE PIONEERS AND OFFER 'OVAL OFFICE TIME'

President Donald Trump highlighted NASA's plans to launch a nuclear-powered spacecraft to Mars within two years during remarks at the UP Summit at Kennedy Space Center in Florida. (Eric Lee/Reuters)

"We're going to be paving the way for astronauts to plant the Stars and Stripes on the red planet," he said.

The planned mission comes as NASA's Artemis program aims to establish a long-term human presence on the moon before eventually sending astronauts to Mars.

Earlier this year, astronauts completed a lunar flyby during the Artemis II mission. Artemis III is scheduled to test spacecraft and landing systems in Earth orbit in 2027, while Artemis IV is expected to attempt a lunar landing in early 2028, before the end of Trump's presidential term.

PETER NAVARRO: TRUMP'S ARTEMIS VISION IS NOW FLYING — AND CHINA IS PAYING ATTENTION

NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman presents President Donald Trump with a plaque while astronauts greet him during NASA's Max Power event at the Kennedy Space Center, in Merritt Island, Fla., on Nov. 6, 2026. (Joe Skipper/Reuters)

Trump also outlined his administration's plans to accelerate space exploration and establish a lasting American presence beyond Earth, saying the U.S. "will establish a permanent presence on lunar soil."

The president pointed to his recently issued American Declaration of Freedom in Space, which asserts the right of Americans to explore and settle beyond Earth under U.S. law.

"Just this week, I issued the American Declaration of Freedom in Space, proclaiming all of space opened for American settlement, American civilization and American freedom," Trump said.

Before introducing the president, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman also discussed the space agency's Mars ambitions and long-term goals.

CHINA IS RACING TO THE MOON, AND AMERICA MUST CLEAR THE PATH FOR SPACE INNOVATION

A "selfie" taken by NASA's Perseverance Mars rover, made up of 62 individual images, on July 23, in this image released Sept. 10, 2025. (NASA handout via Reuters)

"We are going to venture farther into the solar system and move closer to that next giant leap," Isaacman said.

He described the administration's ultimate goal as the day "American astronauts plant the Stars and Stripes on Mars."

The administration has previously outlined broader space ambitions, including plans for a U.S. Space Academy, which Trump announced in August. The proposed institution would train students for careers in the Space Force, NASA and the civilian spaceflight industry.

Trump also emphasized his broader vision for American space exploration.

The lunar surface fills the frame in sharp detail, as seen during the Artemis II lunar flyby, while a distant Earth sets in the background. This image was captured at 6:41 p.m. EDT April 6, 2026, just three minutes before the Orion spacecraft and its crew went behind the Moon and lost contact with Earth for 40 minutes before emerging on the other side. (NASA)

"Our calling is to explore. Our purpose is to build, and our aim is always to win," he said.

#6038. The Dangers of a Lame-Duck Trump

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL W. DREZNER is Academic Dean and Distinguished Professor of International Politics at the Fletcher School of Law and Diplomacy at Tufts University and the author of the newsletter Drezner’s World.

ELIZABETH N. SAUNDERS is Professor of Political Science at Columbia University, a Nonresident Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution, and the author of The Insiders’ Game: How Elites Make War and Peace.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

With the midterms over, U.S. President Donald Trump is now a lame duck. Second-term presidents who lose control of Congress have to face the reality that the time for signature legislation or other domestic policy initiatives has passed. As they think about their legacy, lame ducks often turn to foreign policy, where the power of the presidency allows them to act unfettered by pesky domestic politics. It is not unusual for a lame duck to reach for a final, lofty goal abroad. U.S. President Bill Clinton, for instance, put great effort into trying to advance a proposal for a final settlement to the Israeli-Palestinian conflict at the end of his presidency.

Trump’s lame-duck period will not be like those of his predecessors, however. He has already gone through what was widely considered at the time to be a lame-duck phase, between November 2020 and January 2021. He used it to contest the election outcome despite clear evidence that Joe Biden had won. Trump is the first president in the past 100 years to have served two nonconsecutive terms, and therefore the only American leader in living memory to have a second lame-duck period—this time lasting more than two years.

With Democrats soon to be in control of the House and the Senate, the threat of impeachment or investigations into the Trump family’s business dealings will likely make the president feel cornered and angry. And that’s what makes the remainder of Trump’s term so dangerous. A boxed-in Trump, with no obvious constructive foreign policy outlet to channel his energy, may well lash out internationally. And Congress, even if controlled by Democrats, will have little power over his day-to-day management of foreign policy.

There are plenty of ways Trump could act out: issuing fresh threats against Canada, launching new attacks on U.S. alliances, or colluding with Russia or China at the expense of Ukraine or Taiwan. But to turn those threats into policy would require serious effort, such as repositioning U.S. troops or painstakingly negotiating with other great powers.

The two places where Trump can inflict the most damage are Iran and Cuba. According to The New York Times, the Trump administration and Israel have drawn up plans for fresh strikes on Iran as soon as the midterms are over. A last-ditch effort to compel Tehran to surrender is unlikely to work and would put at risk the global economy and the security and stability of Persian Gulf allies. Trump might also try to force regime change in Cuba. Military action against both adversaries would only require Trump to give his say-so. With no election on the horizon, all that constrains the president abroad is the sense of his own legacy.

We define presidents as lame-duck when they no longer need to contend with elections, either presidential or midterm. For a one-term president who has lost a reelection bid, this period lasts just a few months, between their successor’s election and inauguration. For a second-term president, it can last two years, between the midterms and when they leave office. (Trump, who has never been particularly interested in the political fortunes of other Republicans, is unlikely to let the 2028 presidential election affect his thinking.)

Freed from the burdens of campaigning and legislating, and often lacking domestic political capital, lame-duck leaders tend to pivot to diplomacy, taking chances abroad that they would not have dared to before. Clinton tried to broker an Israeli-Palestinian peace agreement and arrange a presidential trip to North Korea at the end of his second term. After losing the 2014 midterms, President Barack Obama concluded the Iran nuclear deal and normalized relations with Cuba.

Lame-duck presidents have also used military force in ways they might not have done in the lead-up to elections. George H. W. Bush, for example, intervened in Somalia in late 1992 with a limited mission to ease the famine. More than a decade later, in 2007, George W. Bush sent a surge of forces to stabilize Iraq after a punishing midterm election in which voters seemed to send the message that he should withdraw from the failing war there.

The results of these foreign policy moonshots have been mixed. Clinton’s Israeli-Palestinian negotiations failed; Obama’s diplomatic wins were short-lived; George H. W. Bush’s Somalia mission was largely successful in staving off hunger but left the roots of the conflict unresolved, tempting Clinton to expand the mission with disastrous consequences; and George W. Bush’s Iraq surge quelled violence in the short term but did little to permanently stabilize the country. These endeavors were, at the very least, debated and planned with input from relevant parts of government and outside experts. Their aims were clear, even if unmet. And in each case the president attempted to explain and justify his policies—standards Trump has routinely failed to meet.

With Trump now diminished by the midterms yet still on a quest to win a Nobel Peace Prize, one might expect him to turn to diplomacy. The problem is that he has already exhausted many of the obvious lame-duck gambits. During his first term, Trump swung for the foreign policy fences by pulling out of the 2015 Iran nuclear deal, meeting North Korean leader Kim Jong Un three times, replacing the North American Free Trade Agreement with his own regional trade pact, facilitating the normalization of ties between Israel and a handful of Arab countries, and withdrawing from a raft of international agreements. Since returning to office in 2025, he has presided over a cease-fire in Gaza and the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro.

Trump is not expected to return to most of these initiatives because he has reached the limits of what his spectacle-driven diplomacy can achieve. Trump vowed to take Greenland, for example, but has settled for a deal that expands the U.S. military presence in the territory. His “Board of Peace,” which he pitched as a rival to the United Nations Security Council, will continue to languish as other countries now feel less need to kowtow to his every whim.

There are other long shots Trump could attempt, such as trying to end intractable conflicts in Kashmir or Sudan, to forge a reputation as a peacemaker. But these are exceptionally difficult problems to solve in the best of circumstances. Even forcing a lopsided deal in these cases would require a level of deftness, regional expertise, and patience that Trump lacks. He rarely invests meaningful time in diplomacy, as illustrated by his dubious claims to have ended a slew of conflicts, many of which are still ongoing.

Perhaps the biggest swing Trump could take would be to fashion a great-power concert—a forum in which he, Russian President Vladimir Putin, and Chinese leader Xi Jinping worked together to impose global order. As with most of his prior meetings with Putin and Xi, however, future summits are unlikely to produce anything of substance. Neither Russia nor China has expressed much interest in the trilateral nuclear arms treaty that Trump has floated. Nor are they keen on pressing Iran to acquiesce to a deal with the United States.

Trump could use those meetings to throw Ukraine or Taiwan under the bus in the desperate hope of a peace deal. But as the past two years have demonstrated, Putin is not interested in compromise and has the resources to keep fighting. Xi views Taiwan as a domestic Chinese matter and will be unlikely to accept any constraints on his approach to the island. Trump will eventually figure out that he’s better off capitalizing on the optics of a summit of the three leaders than putting in the work to reach any kind of agreement.

Trump is most likely to take action in Iran. He seems to believe that escalating the war could break the stalemate and rescue his legacy. But Iranian leaders won’t back down in the face of more force. Instead, they will probably ramp up attacks on commercial shipping, strike Gulf allies, and ignore diplomatic overtures until Trump leaves office. Moreover, Trump’s own options may be limited: a more radical U.S. escalation against Iran, such as deploying troops to Kharg Island, would probably be so unpopular within his base that he would avoid it. Even Trump’s biggest supporters are growing tired of the high prices that his war has caused. Renewed conflict could plunge the world into economic crisis.

Trump could also attempt to force regime change in Cuba. He has imposed a full-blown embargo on the island for most of 2026 in an effort to weaken its defenses. Cut off from its Venezuelan lifeline and without electricity for all but a few hours a day, the economic hardships and human suffering in Cuba have dramatically worsened in recent months. Ousting Cuba’s government is a personal priority of Marco Rubio, Trump’s secretary of state and national security adviser, and would align with the “Donroe Doctrine,” an assertion of U.S. primacy in the Western Hemisphere.

For Trump, an attack on Cuba would be attractive because it would not require too much in the way of military assets and, at least in his mind, would solve a problem that has bedeviled U.S. presidents stretching back to the end of the Eisenhower administration. Trump might have already moved on Cuba had he not gotten bogged down in Iran. Of course, as the political scientist Alexander Downes has shown, using force to achieve regime change rarely works and often leads to unpredictable consequences—in the case of Cuba, it might mean a new influx of refugees to the United States.

But a lame-duck Trump could see it as a gamble worth taking on the off chance that he can be the president to finally unseat the communist regime. When it comes to foreign policy, Trump loves nothing more than attempting to hack open a Gordian knot. He might also view regime change in Cuba as a way to distract from his failure in Iran.

Another possibility is that Trump will focus his policy efforts abroad on enriching his family. He has already created ample opportunities for foreign entities to seemingly buy influence in his administration. Trump and his family members have accepted a jetliner from the emir of Qatar, a $2 billion investment from a fund led by the Saudi crown prince, and profits from a number of cryptocurrency enterprises, much of which came from foreign buyers. According to The New Yorker, Trump’s family has reaped $4 billion through such profiteering. Trump, secure in the knowledge that the Senate will not have the 67 votes it needs to remove him from office, might figure that he has nothing to lose in pursuing more moneymaking schemes.

The most surprising foreign policy option Trump could take during his lame-duck period, however, is doing nothing at all. The president has busied himself with remaking Washington, D.C., in his own image by redecorating the Oval Office, designing a White House ballroom, and attempting to remodel the Kennedy Center. There is a chance that he could spend his last two years in office issuing pardons, trying to prosecute political enemies on the side, and erecting monuments to himself. Given the alternatives, inaction on foreign policy may well be the best-case scenario for Trump’s lame-duck years.

American Foreign Policy in an Age of Unrestrained Executive Power

The Unconstrained Presidency and the End of American Primacy

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

The Arab Monarchies Must Help Washington and Tehran Save Face

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

#6034. The New Congress Can Repair Civil-Military Relations

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PETER D. FEAVER is Professor of Political Science and Public Policy at Duke University and the author of Thanks for Your Service: The Causes and Consequences of Public Confidence in the U.S. Military.

HEIDI URBEN is Professor of the Practice and Associate Director of Georgetown University’s Center for Security Studies in the Walsh School of Foreign Service.

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

Article I of the Constitution gives Congress sweeping powers to raise, maintain, and equip the armed forces. But the current Congress has done little to use these powers. The intense, frenetic activity of the past two years has undone established norms for military professionalism and may shape civil-military relations for a generation. Yet it has been led almost entirely by the executive branch with little meaningful involvement or pushback from Capitol Hill.

The next Congress will face great pressure to reverse course and in doing so could shore up the historical norms of American civil-military relations. The concern today is not a military that is defying civilians—the traditional bogeyman of American political leaders since the founding of the Republic. Rather, the fear is that the thousands of changes, large and small, that the executive branch has so rapidly imposed on the military may end up undermining the professional ethic on which the U.S. form of civilian control depends.

The incoming Congress can push U.S. civil-military relations in a healthier direction by overseeing the executive branch more actively than its predecessor. It can also help restore trust with both the military and the American public if it focuses on providing greater transparency, takes a more assertive role in conducting defense policy, reinforces the military’s nonpartisan professional ethic, and establishes a new bipartisan commission to reinvigorate the military profession. Congress has the power and authority to improve civil-military relations. The new session, starting in January, is its chance to use them before the damage becomes irreversible.

When Pete Hegseth was sworn in as defense secretary, in 2025, he had strong views on the changes he wanted to make, alleging the military had lost its warfighting focus because of misguided priorities and “wokeism.” He has pursued those changes with a zeal not seen since Robert McNamara held the office from 1961 to 1968. Hegseth has reshaped the senior officer ranks: he has fired or removed nearly two dozen senior general and flag officers, and he has gotten rid of more than one-quarter of all four-star generals, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the chiefs of the U.S. Air Force, Army, and Navy. He has fired, removed, or pulled from promotion lists approximately 80 general and flag officers, a disproportionate number of whom are women or racial minorities. Hegseth has justified those removals in the vaguest of terms, which has convinced most observers that the real reason amounts to little more than because these officers followed the lawful orders of the previous administration. No previous defense secretary has removed such large swaths of the officer corps who honored their oaths to the Constitution and faithfully implemented policies decided by their civilian superiors.

Hegseth has moved quickly, largely ignoring traditional bureaucratic practices, including rigorous staff reviews and close involvement of congressional stakeholders in changing the trajectory of the Pentagon’s policymaking and in conducting military operations. Amid ongoing questions about the legality of the boat strikes the Trump administration ordered against suspected drug traffickers last fall as well as civilian casualties in America’s war with Iran, it is critical to note that Hegseth had already fired the senior, most experienced military legal advisers—sending a warning shot across the bow of younger judge advocates—and begun a significant overhaul of the Judge Advocate General Corps. Ever since the My Lai massacre during the Vietnam War, when U.S. soldiers committed war crimes by killing hundreds of unarmed Vietnamese civilians, the JAG Corps had been empowered to help U.S. personnel abide by the laws of armed conflict. By demoting senior legal advisers and selecting less qualified personnel in those positions, however, the military’s legal voice is more marginalized today than it has been since the Vietnam War. The Trump administration has also centralized decision-making, including by placing routine actions in highly classified channels that minimize opportunities for input and deliberation.

Hegseth has done all of this—and much more—with essentially no pushback from the uniformed military or Pentagon bureaucracy. On the one hand, this shows just how strongly officers accept the principle of civilian control—a posture reinforced precisely because Hegseth has proved willing to punish even unsupported allegations of military insubordination. On the other hand, his actions have had a chilling effect across the force, creating an environment in which the military is reluctant to offer candid advice or uphold its professional standards and values when they are at odds with administration priorities. Indeed, in a highly unusual move, former government officials have publicly called for military leaders to do more to stand up to the Trump administration, even at the cost of undermining civilian control. As we argued in Foreign Affairs in 2024, however, the military cannot save democracy; only Congress and the courts can and should check presidential power.

Hegseth has enjoyed the unwavering support of President Donald Trump. But the real secret to Hegseth’s success in imposing his agenda on the Pentagon has been the unwillingness of the current Congress to exercise its Article I responsibilities or defend its traditional prerogatives. The incoming Congress can take a different approach by using its power to build trust, transparency, and accountability.

Most pressing, Congress should exercise its ability to appropriately scrutinize defense policymaking, much of which has been shrouded in secrecy under the Trump administration. The current Congress has held very few oversight hearings, and the ones that have taken place have been exchanges of insults rather than of information. When secrecy is genuinely needed, of course, Congress can issue classified briefings and hold closed hearings. But open hearings, when properly conducted, are necessary in cases in which Congress is entitled to answers, for example, about military personnel policy.

In the case of the boat strikes in the Caribbean and the eastern Pacific, Congress needs to know what advice senior military commanders and their JAGs offered, and it must understand how JAG firings affect the process by which commanders discern the lawfulness of military operations. Thus far, the Defense Department has provided only superficial explanations of its actions that basically amount to “we did this because we thought it was necessary.” Such justifications fall far short of what the Constitution envisions as the best way to hold the armed forces accountable: open debate that subjects policies to rigorous scrutiny. Greater transparency would also bolster public confidence in defense policy and remove pressure on the military to have to act as a potential savior of the Constitution.

Congress should also faithfully fulfill its “advice and consent” function established in Article II, which authorizes the Senate to review and confirm presidential nominations and promotions. Hegseth’s unprecedented intervention in the meritocratic promotion process has dramatically altered the military’s upper echelons. Congress has normalized this interference, perhaps unwittingly, by approving Hegseth’s altered promotion lists with voice votes and little inquiry.

The president and the defense secretary must retain the right to remove officers in whom they have lost confidence. But Congress has the right to know why a change was made, and it needs to ensure that promotions and removals follow established law and procedures rather than ideological or partisan considerations. Because Congress created the process for meritocratic promotions, it can push the executive branch to adhere to that process. It should mandate that the defense secretary provide a written explanation, supported by documented evidence of misconduct or derogatory information, for any off-cycle removals of senior officers and restore senior officers’ promotions, which would limit unjustified removals and uphold the merit-based selection process. Improving transparency in personnel policy will have one further salutary effect: it will help retain those qualified military personnel who are wondering whether they should give up on a military career that could be derailed by politicized and capricious promotion decisions.

In addition, Congress has a responsibility to police the well-established principle and practice that the uniformed military must stay away from partisan politics. The military has largely tried to uphold this norm, even as civilian political leaders have sought to draw it into partisan debates. Each time, for instance, Hegseth attacks the Biden administration before military audiences, most of whom served during the Biden era, he unwittingly further degrades the nonpartisan ethic on which service members’ oaths of office—and thus their subordination to his own policies—depend. It sends a message that embracing partisan rhetoric is key to career advancement and signals to the public that the military is not a neutral constituency. Congress can police the executive branch’s politicization of the military through inquiries, hearings, and even measures that prohibit the use of appropriated funds for events that treat military audiences like campaign rallies. Members of Congress can also call out for censure uniformed military personnel who behave in a blatantly partisan fashion regardless of whether they support or oppose the party the member supports.

Most ambitiously, Congress should reclaim its role in making defense policy rather than only providing oversight. Legislative deference to the executive in times of war is not unique to Trump’s second term, but Congress should close loopholes that allow presidents to expand their power unchecked. If Congress wants to provide a legal justification to allow the use of force in Iran or against drug traffickers in the Caribbean or the eastern Pacific Ocean, it should do so. If it believes these actions are unlawful, however, it must use its legislative powers to constrain them. Similarly, Congress should amend the Insurrection Act to clarify and restrict the domestic use of military force. The president must retain the ability to respond to true national emergencies, but Congress should narrow the criteria for deployment of troops on U.S. soil to avoid potential abuse.

Even as it becomes more active, Congress must strenuously avoid involving the military in a partisan war. Members of Congress should not press the military to criticize the president or the defense secretary in the made-for-social-media-outrage hearings that have become commonplace in Washington. It is right and proper for Congress to ask members of the military to share their personal and professional opinions on key strategic questions. But too often, politicians try to turn officers into combatants in their partisan battles, hiding behind the armed forces to defend partisan agendas or attacking military leaders who have simply carried out the lawful orders of a president from another party. Above all, Congress should not ask the military to be a check on civilian power. Only other civilians have the authority to do that.

Congress cannot solve longer-term issues through the day-to-day legislative process. But it has other tools to more holistically improve civil-military relations and identify the most essential reforms. The time-honored strategy is to set up a nonpartisan blue-ribbon commission to analyze problems, draw attention to their importance, and recommend solutions. A national commission on the state of the professional military ethic, co-chaired by three respected individuals—one with experience in a Republican administration, one with experience in a Democratic administration, and one retired senior military leader—would have the freedom to investigate how recent developments, including attempts to politicize the military and sustain a high tempo of operations, have affected the force. At a minimum, the commission should explore impacts on recruiting and retention; professional military education, fellowships in civilian institutions, and general and flag officer development; the JAG Corps’ ability to provide commanders with candid, independent legal advice; and the state of the National Guard.

The current Congress has not shown enough interest in exercising its own powers. The next one can make a clean break and rediscover the body’s ability to carry out the functions that are necessary to improve civil-military relations. The new Congress can demonstrate its commitment to fulfilling its responsibilities by providing ordinary legislative oversight in the raising, maintaining, and equipping of the armed forces. And on thorny issues that require a bigger response, Congress can start the reform process by creating a national commission to lay out an agenda for action. Even after two years of enormous strain that has eroded best practices in civil-military relations, the next Congress is well positioned to reverse the slide and shore up Americans’ faith in a nonpartisan, professional military that is essential to democracy.

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

#6033. DeSantis blasts Washington Post over report he's preparing 2028 presidential bid

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis said Wednesday following the midterm elections that Democrats "did not earn a mandate to indulge all their left-wing fantasies."

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis fired back Friday at a report that he is preparing another White House bid in 2028, accusing The Washington Post of fabricating claims that he has started reaching out to political consultants about a potential campaign.

"The Washington Post is fabricating this out of whole cloth," DeSantis wrote on X. "I have not reached out to anybody about running for anything."

"Just another pathetic attempt to garner clicks. Using anonymous sources of course!" he added.

The Post reported Friday, citing three people briefed on his plans, that DeSantis was preparing to seek the Republican presidential nomination in 2028.

VANCE, CRUZ AND DESANTIS DIAGNOSE GOP'S MIDTERM DISASTER AS BATTLE OVER PARTY'S FUTURE BEGINS

Florida Gov. Ron DeSantis speaks during a press conference at the ICE Enforcement and Removal Operations office in Miramar, Florida, on May 1. (Joe Raedle/Getty Images)

#6030. China’s Economics of Denial

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Oliver Melton is a Director with Rhodium Group’s China practice.

Logan Wright is a Partner at Rhodium Group and leads its research on China’s economy and financial markets. He is the author of the book Broken China: How the Economic Miracle Shattered and What it Means for the World.

Beijing’s Theory of Success in the Face of Failure

China has correctly diagnosed its most pressing economic problems. The Chinese Communist Party has acknowledged that its economy relies too much on investment and exports and that persistently weak household consumption is the core threat to future growth. The leadership also knows that this imbalanced growth model is causing trade friction with developed and emerging economies alike and will continue to do so. They know there is a growing chorus of economists (including many quiet voices in China) raising the alarm that, if Beijing does not make major changes soon, China will face ever-slower growth, cascading trade disputes, and potentially stagnating household income.

But what is puzzling is that the country’s leaders are not acting. Beijing has announced only modest plans to boost household consumption and domestic demand, and its overall economic strategy remains essentially unchanged—focused on expanding investment in manufacturing, especially in advanced technologies and new strategic industries.

Theories abound as to why Beijing has chosen to ignore the warnings. Many speculate that Chinese leader Xi Jinping is not receiving accurate information or that he and his closest allies have abandoned development as a priority in favor of national security goals or a desire to control the commanding heights of global industry. Others argue that Beijing knows what it should do, but political or institutional constraints prevent it from taking decisive action.

In truth, while these factors all play a part in shaping policy, the real explanation is far simpler. The party is sticking to this path because the leadership believes it is working. It sees the same troubling data—including rising external surpluses, weak consumption, slower household income growth, weak credit demand, rising unemployment and underemployment, and persistent deflation—but it has reached wildly different conclusions about what these developments indicate and what Beijing should do in response.

Chinese economic policy is nested within a methodical planning process, which is meant to ensure that short-term pressures do not distract from long-term priorities. The problem is that the long-term strategy is predicated on the belief that control of advanced technologies and industry will trigger a new round of high-speed growth. This economic strategy can best be understood as a Marxism-infused misapplication of neoclassical economic development theory: Beijing is obsessed with control over technology and physical capital and believes that its ultrahigh levels of investment will accelerate productivity gains and wage growth. But a disproportionate focus on manufacturing and technology cannot increase productivity or wages if those investments are decoupled from the needs of the market and trends in aggregate final demand. This is especially true in a country as large as China—already the world’s largest exporter—that cannot rely on further exports to fuel growth.

China’s worldview treats development as equivalent to capital accumulation and sees productivity as determined by the control and deployment of technology. It posits that the growth of the service sector, higher wages, and higher consumption are the consequences of that physical underlying foundation—they follow naturally in later stages of the development process once the productive industries have flourished. In this conception of economic development, the right strategy is always more investment, more industry, and more technology. The role of the state is to ensure that household savings and industrial profits are recycled through the state-owned banking system or government-guided capital into priority investments rather than frivolous activities, such as services or consumption. The resulting structural imbalance that weakens household income growth, the service sector, and public services was thus not an accident; in China’s economic development theory, this is seen as self-discipline that will pay off with more rapid long-term development.

A key corollary to this view is that the government’s macroeconomic management must guide resource allocation proactively through hands-on intervention, not impassively through interest rates and exchange rates. The state can harness the dynamism and ambition of markets to promote efficiency and innovation in defined spaces, but it is the government’s job to identify the correct focal points for investment to ensure that priority sectors grow at maximum speed and then to intervene to ameliorate imbalances when they inevitably arise.

In this conception of macroeconomics, the economy consists of a massive input-output table where every cell in the matrix is a sector that consumes other cells’ outputs and provides inputs to other producers. If there is an imbalance in supply or demand for certain products—which becomes evident in high or low prices—it may be because of overinvestment in the producing sector or underinvestment in downstream consuming sectors. Government policy seeks to increase output and steadily lower prices by facilitating investment and production throughout the matrix, adjusting the pace and balance of resource allocation as needed if one cell’s output exceeds the demands of the others. The goal is not to develop overcapacity or low profitability in these targeted sectors, but the optimal path for rapid development will inevitably push sectors in that direction.

Beijing has followed this playbook closely. When, for instance, the real estate sector collapsed in 2022 and the structural imbalances in China’s economy became too big to ignore, Beijing responded by increasing its efforts to micromanage credit flows, reversing experiments with interest rate liberalization and financial market reforms while tightening oversight of capital markets and industrial policy funding. Central bank relending facilities now channel low-cost bank loans to prioritize industrial upgrading and equipment purchases for targeted sectors. Thousands of government-run equity funds provide low-cost capital to firms that support a web of industrial and technology strategies. State-owned enterprises intervene to help boost valuations in the stock market, and regulators prioritize firms in strategic sectors for new listings. When a sector is overinvested, policy campaigns such as “anti-involution”—a neologism that describes a basket of discordant policies to resolve overcapacity—are deployed to slow future investment, buttress profitability by fixing prices, or set standards to restrict market entry in a bid to slow output while the other cells in the input-output tables catch up and restore balance over time.

Although hard to comprehend in the context of a market economy, some aspects of this approach have an understandable internal logic in China, especially at a micro level. The collapse of the property sector—roughly a quarter of GDP at its peak—sent massive shock waves through very specific supply and demand pathways in the economy. It made sense for Beijing to calibrate policy for the affected sectors and think in terms of input-output tables as it designed its responses. The frequency of bubbles in China’s equity, commodity, and property markets over the past 15 years has left policymakers deeply wary of accommodative monetary policy for the entire economy, which helps explain why they are experimenting with more targeted tools aimed at specific sectors.

Likewise, a certain level of triumphalism in Beijing is understandable, given that Chinese companies are breaking into pole positions in major conventional industries, such as autos and telecommunications, and many others are poised to thrive in cutting-edge sectors, such as robotics and biotechnology—all in the wake of a massive hit to GDP. For a generation of economic policymakers who cut their teeth trying to revitalize a moribund planned economy in the 1990s and the first decade of this century, the emergence of world-leading (often private) firms is definitive evidence that Beijing has found a winning recipe. From this perspective, Beijing is also dismissive of foreign complaints about unfair market interventions or trade imbalances as the sour grapes of rich countries that had grown accustomed to rent-seeking technology monopolies.

China’s leaders also see some deflation as the fruits of hard-won efficiency gains: the elimination of bottlenecks, economies of scale in manufacturing clusters, construction of world-class infrastructure, improved logistics, and removal of market barriers that have lowered costs up and down manufacturing supply chains. One-off cost reductions from efficiency gains or the abrupt loss of construction activity must be digested by the economy, they would argue, not offset by stimulus. And while foreign commentators and dignitaries warn of growing trade pushback, the data tells a surprisingly different story: China’s export growth barely slowed as the Trump administration imposed sweeping duties and the European Union set up defenses against a handful of sectors, such as electric vehicles.

The core problem with China’s economic strategy, however, is that aggregate domestic demand consistently falls far short of domestic supply. Chinese planners’ input-output tables will not balance unless domestic workers are buying the goods that China’s industries produce. Otherwise, everything else must be exported, often at lower prices. The leadership understands this in principle but believes that the control of advanced industries and technology will, ipso facto, cause productivity to rise, sustain economic growth into the future, and generate higher wages and therefore higher consumption.

This is the flaw in Beijing’s approach—the dangerous legacy of vestigial Marxism and misapplied neoclassical growth theory. In reality, state intervention in markets can depress overall productivity and workers can see flat wages even when China succeeds at promoting advanced industries.

It is true that capital and technology create productivity gains in well-functioning market economies. But the causality usually breaks down if resources are allocated by policy interventions that are divorced from returns on investment. The control or ownership of advanced industries does not inherently cause economic growth, because they are far too small a share of the economy. Instead, new products or technologies generate productivity when they are distributed and used throughout the economy in ways that improve efficiency or increase output. It is not the success of a robotics firm that leads to efficiency gains, for example, it is the deployment of its robots in factories to save production costs—namely by displacing workers—and boost output. If those benefits do not exceed the true cost of the new industrial robots—even with layers of subsidies—then the net effect is not positive, no matter how impressive the factories themselves are, or how successful the robotics firm itself appears.

The use of new production technologies also requires a degree of creative destruction that Beijing’s own industrial policy interventions have inhibited. When governments, not price-conscious firms, make technology and investment decisions, policymakers are not just gambling that they know better than the market how to boost long-run performance. They are also betting that they know how to use those technologies to create innovative new business models. Yet in China, state influence over resource allocation has ossified markets, especially by reducing bankruptcies, in ways that limit the fruits of innovation and efficiency gains in the broader economy. Additionally, much of China’s industrial policy aims to build self-reliance, which, by definition, focuses on duplicative investments that buttress domestic industrial prowess without necessarily adding efficiency.

Moreover, even though China’s many layers of industrial policy have helped launch successful companies and built an impressive fleet of ultramodern factories, there are also tremendous inefficiencies and waste that never make the headlines. Overall productivity must be calculated comprehensively, evaluating the net output gains against all the industrial subsidies, bad debts, foregone profits, and indirect support for investment that the government has provided. The reality is that China’s aggregate success rate is far lower than the shining stars would suggest: the remarkable global reach of electric vehicle and battery makers such as BYD, CATL, and Li Auto masks the dozens of failed companies that generated nonperforming loans and ate up unprofitable capital subsidies throughout the bulk of the sector and its supply chain. It is far from obvious that the net return on investment from industrial policy spending, estimated by the International Monetary Fund to be up to four percent of GDP, is positive, and it could be deeply negative, despite pockets of success.

Finally, Beijing is betting that superefficient manufacturing and success in strategic industries will eventually lead to higher wages, which will then cause consumption to surge. It is true that productivity growth causes real incomes to rise in a competitive market economy. But if investments in labor-saving technologies are driven by state policy, not returns on investment, wages can fall even as firms become more efficient. Rising household income requires a key intermediate step: employment, which is not a core characteristic of China’s capital-intensive, advanced manufacturing investment strategy. Indeed, state funding has helped China—a middle-income economy—become a world leader in high-tech “dark factories” that operate without distracting human accommodations, such as light. This may increase output in prized manufacturing cells in the input-output tables, but if demand for labor is not growing in aggregate, the whole equation starts to fall apart.

Still, the most pernicious consequence of Beijing’s economic model is entrenched deflation. The focus on expanding production combined with China’s weak consumer demand has caused the producer price index to fall for longer than it has risen over the past decade. This is a far larger threat to China’s economy than a mere nuisance of sectoral imbalances. Deflation reduces private-sector credit demand; undercuts incentives for new investments; and crushes employment, wage expectations, and, therefore, consumer confidence, which leads to weaker domestic demand—not in carefully demarcated input-output relationships but throughout the economy. Deflation also saps fiscal revenues and puts pressure on financial institutions in ways that severely limit the state’s capacity to act decisively the longer it lasts. Deflationary pressures in China also spill over to the global economy through cheaper exports and a weakening real exchange rate, driving more tensions between Beijing and its trading partners.

There is immense risk in falling behind the curve in fighting deflation, but Beijing is not likely to change course if it continues to believe its strategy will boost productivity and jumpstart domestic demand in the long run. Some analysts speculate that Beijing could be forced into a reappraisal of its vision in response to unemployment pressures or the proliferation of foreign trade friction, but it has already shown its cards. The accumulation of unemployment, underemployment, and low wages for Chinese households and workers are already significant, yet Beijing has responded with only modest new household subsidies and incremental investment in social services. Likewise, as external trade tensions have risen, Beijing has managed these country-by-country disputes as diplomatic issues, through a mix of threats and negotiations, to preserve the bulk of China’s export markets.

The decision to pursue short-term Band-Aid measures for a weak labor market and foreign trade tensions is confusing at first pass. For a government laser-focused on self-reliance and eliminating foreign dependencies, it is a curious strategy to become ever more dependent on foreign demand—over which Beijing has little control—as its primary driver of economic growth. Likewise, for a regime that is obsessed with social stability, it would seem prudent to focus more on wages and labor demand. Yet the strategy makes a degree of sense when viewed from the leadership’s perspective that China is in a transitional period. It may be unpleasant and difficult, but it is an unavoidable step on the path to a technology-led growth model.

The leadership’s current confidence in this transition process means that it could be many years before the warning signs, already visible, become persuasive internally. This all but guarantees that Beijing will continue to defer action while deflationary pressures and external trade tensions build. Deflation and weak corporate profitability are already eroding China’s fiscal revenues, which have basically been flat in nominal terms since 2019. Deflation and the growing debt burden are also corroding the financial system, which will ultimately require large write-offs and recapitalization from the government budget to prevent credit growth from slowing further. A $54 billion bank recapitalization just took place two months ago, with little effect on lending.

Beijing’s ideological commitment to its industry- and technology-focused development strategy is creating a dangerous cycle that will likely produce weaker consumption, slower income growth, and even greater trade friction with the rest of the world. Moreover, in the face of such challenges, China’s economic leadership is compensating by expanding state intervention in the economy in ways that further undermine market forces and the independence of the private sector. At this rate, even if the leadership eventually recognizes the need to change strategy, China may not have the fiscal or financial space to rebalance the economy toward a more sustainable and diversified growth model.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., and Gov. JB Pritzker, D-Ill., said the American people deserve an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency while leaving The Center for American Progress. (Credit: Nicholas Ballasy for Fox News Digital)

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., and Gov. JB Pritzker, D-Ill., are calling for an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE), previously led by Elon Musk, as talks have heated up among Democrats to launch a probe into the multibillionaire.

"I had no specific plan," Raskin told Fox News Digital about the potential investigations. "Look…DOGE was a catastrophe for America, and at least one committee, I feel pretty certain, will investigate it. It needs to investigate it."

Musk has emerged as a potential target of Democratic oversight, with some lawmakers calling for scrutiny of his time as head of the DOGE.

The calls for an investigation come as Democrats have started rallying the party to look into corruption under the Trump administration after coming out victorious in midterms.

SCHUMER, JEFFRIES SIDESTEP QUESTIONS ON NUKING FILIBUSTER, LAUNCHING ELON MUSK PROBE AFTER BLUE WAVE

Democrats are calling for an investigation into the Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE), previously led by Elon Musk. (Getty Images, File)

The tech CEO helped launch DOGE at the start of President Donald Trump's second term to root out waste and be more transparent about the federal government's spending.

DOGE's efforts included deferred resignations and buyouts of employees at federal agencies and contributed to a significant downsizing of the federal workforce, which contracted by about 250,000 employees during 2025, according to the Government Accountability Office (GAO).

Calls for investigations into DOGE also started over concerns about the department allegedly having access to Americans’ sensitive information.

"I mean the whole thing was premised on a lie, and our private information was completely compromised by it," Raskin said.

Despite Pritzker saying he had never "heard anything like that" about a potential probe of Musk and DOGE, he agreed that DOGE is worthy of an investigation.

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker agrees that DOGE is worthy of an investigation. (Terrence Antonio James/Chicago Tribune/Tribune News Service via Getty Images, File)

"I think there should be a lot of inspection of what DOGE did," Pritzker told Fox News Digital. "I think that was pretty awful. "

But Pritzker didn’t seem so sure that needing an investigation into DOGE also warranted an investigation into Musk himself.

"I don't know about focusing on one person," Pritzker said when asked about investigating Musk.

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested looking into Musk’s companies' multibillion-dollar contracts with the federal government and, more recently, his role in cutting internet access to the Ukrainian military.

After the midterm elections resulted in a sweeping blue wave, Democrats in both chambers of Congress have vowed to use investigative committees and subpoena powers to probe not only Musk, but Trump and much of his administration — including trying to impeach Trump.

#6027. Don’t Soak the Rich

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Daniel WALDENSTROM is Professor of Economics at the Research Institute of Industrial Economics in Stockholm. He is the author of Richer and More Equal: A New History of Wealth in the West.

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

As private wealth continues to grow to levels never before seen in modern history, a powerful idea is taking hold in the West: the accumulation of great fortunes poses an existential threat to democracy. This idea is not novel. Over a decade ago, the political scientists Jacob Hacker and Paul Pierson asserted that the rise of plutocracy has been a defining trend of American politics since the late 1970s. The economist Paul Krugman argued in 2011 that the “extreme concentration of income is incompatible with real democracy.”

Lately, however, it has begun to gain traction in the halls of power of Western democracies. In his farewell address, former U.S. President Joe Biden argued that an “oligarchy of extreme wealth, power, and influence” was threatening “our entire democracy, our basic rights and freedoms.” Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez echoed the sentiment at a 2025 G-20 summit, warning that concentrated wealth “corrodes democratic institutions.”

Some now see confiscation as the answer. After a bitter debate and over $300 million of cumulative spending by supporters and opponents, Californians rejected a statewide “billionaires’ tax” ballot measure in this week’s midterm elections. But the proposal counted among its proponents six Nobel laureates in economics, including Daron Acemoglu, Peter Diamond, and Joseph Stiglitz, who endorsed the tax in a letter framing it as “a turning point in the battle between democracy and oligarchy.” The French economist Gabriel Zucman called the measure’s defeat “just the beginning” and vowed that though “Silicon Valley billionaires have won this battle … they will lose this war.” Several European political leaders, including the former German Green Party leader Annalena Baerbock and Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen, have floated wealth tax proposals of their own in recent years.

The risk they have identified is genuine. Democratic societies are not doing enough to prevent the superrich from unduly influencing politics. But their diagnosis—not to mention their preferred solution—is incorrect. Billionaires’ participation in politics does not in itself indicate that a democracy has been captured by moneyed interests. The strength of the political and civil institutions meant to protect against the exercise of those interests is a far more important indicator.

Instead of imposing wealth taxes, countries that are serious about safeguarding their political systems should take their cues from those democracies that have successfully insulated their politics from the influence of large fortunes. They should adopt transparent election laws and restraints on private campaign contributions and encourage a strong, independent media. Such measures will help make societies more democratic. Taxing wealth will simply make them poorer.

Arguments in favor of taxing wealth in the name of democracy often hinge on an understanding of billionaires as a monolithic bloc, committed to an agenda that runs counter to the public interest. These claims contain a kernel of truth. According to the benchmark V-Dem and Freedom House indices, the United States has indeed been in a state of democratic decline since the early 2010s. But it does not follow that billionaires as a group are responsible for this decline—or that such a unified group even exists.

To empirically determine its existence, I classified the richest Americans in the 2026 Forbes 400 listing according to their traceable political donations, analyzing both their average annual giving and their total giving over the past three decades. This method is no doubt imperfect—donations can be informal or hidden and are not the only way to achieve political influence—but the pattern that emerges should nonetheless cast doubt on the idea of a single billionaire class marching on Washington.

The evidence shows that roughly 30 individuals, or eight percent of U.S. billionaires in the Forbes 400, are high-level donors, giving more than $1 million each year for political purposes. Roughly half of American billionaires give on average less than $30,000 a year, 35 percent give less than $10,000, and 15 percent, or 60 of the listed billionaires, have no visible political footprint. Just 16 donors—four percent—have given more than $50 million over the last 30 years. This small minority may well plow enough money into elections to make up for the majority of billionaires with no apparent interest in politics (although if Elon Musk’s midterm outlay is any indication, even vast sums of money are no guarantee of political outcomes). But the very fact that both these groups exist suggests that the presence of the ultrawealthy alone is not necessarily responsible for American democratic decay. The weakness of the institutions meant to check their influence, on the other hand, is.

For the American billionaires who do engage in politics, federal rules and institutions make it easy to achieve disproportionate influence through large-scale private spending. Super PACs are permitted to receive unlimited contributions from individuals, corporations, unions, and other political committees. The hollowing out of independent public-service media and the consolidation of private media make matters worse, allowing a small number of wealthy owners to shape the dominant narrative unchecked.

Much of the momentum behind a wealth tax comes from the United States. But it is hardly the only country with a high proportion of superwealthy residents. Sweden and Switzerland have among the most billionaires per capita in the world, with around 4.7 and 5.0 billionaires per million inhabitants, respectively—higher than the United States’ 2.9 and dwarfing the average of 1.3 for Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries. If wealth tax proponents were correct about the relationship between extreme wealth and democratic health, one would expect both Sweden’s and Switzerland’s democracies to be in dire shape.

But in neither country has the presence of large private fortunes significantly damaged democracy. Both rank highly among liberal democracies when assessing the fairness of elections, the rule of law, checks on the executive, trust in government, and media freedom. Sweden ranks fifth in the 2026 World Press Freedom Index, and Switzerland ranks eighth. As of 2025, they were tied for sixth in Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index; the United States ranked 29th. According to the OECD’s 2025 trust survey, 62 percent of Swiss respondents had confidence in their national government, the highest of all countries polled. Sweden, at 42 percent, ranked 15th among the countries polled, higher than the OECD average of 40 percent and far exceeding the United States’ 27 percent, as measured by the Partnership for Public Service.

It might be tempting to point to tax policy as an explanation. Indeed, social democratic Sweden, with its 41 percent tax-to-GDP ratio as of 2024, has one of the world’s highest tax levels. Switzerland, however, maintains a tax-to-GDP ratio of roughly 27 percent, well below the OECD average of 34 percent and about even with the U.S. level of 25 percent. That these countries can support large entrepreneurial fortunes without sacrificing their democracies is in fact not the result of draconian restrictions on how those fortunes are spent in politics. Two other shared institutional features offer more compelling answers.

In Sweden, political parties receive funding mainly through public structures. Private contributions are allowed, but individual donors represent a relatively small share of total party funding, and disclosure rules make all large private contributions visible. Sweden’s constitution grants citizens and journalists the right to access almost any official document. Ministers hold little direct influence over administrative agencies, allowing the agencies to operate independently of executive authority. These rules do not eliminate lobbying, but they largely prevent private money from discreetly entering and influencing the political process.

Switzerland’s barriers between private wealth and the levers of government, although similarly sturdy, stop money from influencing politics in slightly different ways. The country’s federal system diffuses power across 26 regional cantons and over 2,000 municipalities, and frequent regional and federal referendums give citizens veto power over legislation. Switzerland has no direct federal funding of parties and no general spending ceiling, but national disclosure rules introduced in 2022 require major campaigns and large donors to identify their financing. The country does have a wealth tax dating back to the nineteenth century, but it is administered at the canton level and its highest marginal rate is nowhere near as punitive as the California proposal. Dispersing authority and enshrining accountability over government decisions through direct democracy are far more responsible for the success of Swiss democracy.

Both countries also have long traditions of private journalism and independent public-service media. A mix of private outlets and public-service media, which has been shown to foster greater public trust in the news and resilience to online disinformation, prevents any single news provider from monopolizing the airwaves or suppressing information.

These institutions are not perfect, and private donations still play a role in Swedish and Swiss politics. That countries with nearly twice the number of billionaires per capita as the United States have thriving democracies should, however, give the lie to the idea that the superrich, by their mere existence, deform democracy.

The proposals currently in vogue are presented as a novel solution to the novel problem of concentrated wealth. But societies have attempted to tax wealth before. Throughout the twentieth century, many European countries imposed similarly designed taxes on household net wealth for redistributive purposes. Problems quickly emerged. Taxing individuals who rarely generated cash income and whose fortunes were thus not easily estimated outraged entrepreneurs, lowered investment, and hampered growth. In the 1970s and 1980s, governments on both the left and the right took note. They tried to discount certain business assets and exempt closely held firms from taxation to mitigate the distortions. But tinkering around these edges only caused new problems of unfairness. Revenues, which at that time constituted less than half a percent of GDP across Europe, compared with total taxes that often stood at 40 percent of GDP, slumped. Ultimately, most countries scrapped the tax.

Many of today’s proponents are more likely to cite the threat of unprecedented fortunes to democracy than the need for redistribution as the impetus for a wealth tax. But they offer no new solutions to the problems of old. Nor would a wealth tax stop billionaires from financing politics, since the wealthiest hardly need to throw the full weight of their fortunes around to wield political influence.

Supporters might argue that the pro-democracy case for a wealth tax is much broader in scope. After all, the superrich exert influence on politics in more ways than just their donations. A wealth tax might not stop a billionaire from buying a state election, but it might stop one from, for example, buying Paramount or The Washington Post. Imperfect a solution as it is, a billionaires’ tax referendum represents a more achievable goal than amending the Constitution to wrest power from the executive.

But a wealth tax has never been an efficient fiscal tool or a substitute for substantive democratic renewal. Whatever small democratic gains it might feasibly bring would be outweighed by the threat to business ventures, capital formation, and long-term economic development it would pose. As anything more than a symbolic measure, a tax on uncertain, often illiquid values is essentially a tax on entrepreneurial risk-taking. But the case against it should not be mistaken for a broadside against all capital taxation. Governments should still seek to raise substantial revenue from taxes on the returns on capital and should set rates and brackets how they please. They must not, however, forget the crucial distinction between taxing already realized income and taxing assets that may yet generate new returns.

A better agenda to safeguard democracy against the influence of money should prioritize reforming the institutions whose weaknesses make such influence possible. Limits on campaign and party finance and more transparent disclosure of lobbying and conflicts of interest would, at the very least, make elections more difficult to buy. Increased funding for public media and stricter antitrust rules to block media mergers would create a more diverse landscape of both private and independent public-service outlets. These measures would affect just the few American billionaires who plow significant parts of their fortunes into politics, leaving the majority of the country’s wealthiest to keep doing what they do best—creating value and driving growth.

Democracies should, of course, always fear oligarchy. They should actively work to curtail the influence of money on politics. They should not, however, confuse economic success with political capture. Countries need entrepreneurs who are willing to take risks and build wealth. Democracy will survive in an era of extraordinary fortunes not by antagonizing successful people but by making political power harder to buy.

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

#6026. 4 of nation's largest cities now led by socialist mayors after Tuesday's election, affecting tens of millions

'The Big Money Show' panel analyzes the market rally after Democrats take control of Congress. The panel says the key election issues were based on affordability and immigration.

Two socialist mayors were elected in the United States on Tuesday in Los Angeles and Washington, D.C. which now means that four of the top 20 largest cities in the country have socialist leaders at the helm.

Los Angeles Councilwoman Nithya Raman, a member of the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA) defeated incumbent mayor Karen Bass and will take office in December as mayor for the roughly 4 million residents of Los Angeles proper.

Also victorious on Tuesday was DSA member Janeese Lewis George who coasted to victory to become mayor-elect of Washington, D.C., a city of roughly 700,000 residents.

The list of socialist mayors in the top 20 most populous U.S. cities stands at 4, as George and Raman join Seattle Mayor Katie Wilson and New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani.

DEMOCRATIC SOCIALIST SWEEPS INTO DC MAYOR'S OFFICE, SIGNALS OPENNESS TO WORKING WITH TRUMP

Former Biden White House aide Yemisi Egbewole predicted that the Democratic Socialists of America would struggle to gain national influence ahead of the 2028 presidential election despite recent electoral victories. (2019 Getty Images)

In total, the four cities are home to roughly 14 million people. That figure does not include neighboring suburbs, which could still feel the effects of policies enacted within city limits through crime, real estate values and the broader business climate. Problems in a city can spill over into surrounding communities, while suburban residents who work in the city may also be directly affected.

Counting the population in the metro areas of those four cities, roughly 44 million people could be affected either positively and negatively by the socialist policies enacted by the four mayors.

"Chicago's mayor is a buffoon, but he doesn't identify as DSA. America used to be an impregnable fortress against Marxism, then Marxists took over universities."

The socialist victories were not limited to city halls. Voters also elected seven DSA-backed or affiliated candidates to the House: Claire Valdez, Brand Lander and Darializa Avila Chevalier of New York, Chris Rabb of Pennsylvania, Melat Kiros of Colorado, Donavan McKinney of Michigan and Angela Gonzales-Torres of California.

Several other candidates associated with socialist policies also entered Congress or were re-elected on Tuesday.

Family members and supporters of D.C. Ward 4 Council member Janeese Lewis George, a candidate for D.C. mayor, cheer as she addresses them at Prince Hall Masonic Temple, a polling location, in Washington, D.C., on Monday, June 8, 2026. (The Washington Post via Getty Images)

A recent Fox News national poll indicated 32% of voters have a favorable view of socialism, with 64% seeing it in an unfavorable way. But the favorable rating matches an all-time high, first reached five years ago, since Fox News first began asking the question in 2019. And a September Gallup poll found that 43% of Americans had a positive image of socialism, the highest level since the polling organization began tracking the question in 2010.

The Fox News poll found that 51% of voters had a favorable view of capitalism, compared with 32% who viewed socialism favorably. Another 46% had an unfavorable view of capitalism.
