#5910. China Truck Sales Now Majority Electric. What’s Next?

EV heavy trucks hit 50.3% market share in China for September, with sales up 34% from a year ago. Long-haul big rig market share hit 64.5%. China is on track to have over 40% EV market share for the entire year, reaching their 2030 goal in a matter of months. This is not the first time China’s heavy truck market share topped 50%, but that previous blip in December last year was prior to subsidies ending, followed by a dip in January. Current sales rates are not bolstered by the subsidy and are now on a much more stable upward trajectory.

The speed of EV adoption for heavy trucks in China has recently grown much faster than for cars, having risen from just 13.6% in 2024. When I visited Guangzhou in November of last year and again in April of this year, I noticed a lot more green plates on trucks, indicating that they were electric. It seems that my perception is backed by sales data.

With the addition of LNG trucks, diesel’s share of heavy trucks has dropped to roughly 40%. While LNG sales have also declined with the rate of EV adoption, diesel trucks could soon make up less than 1/3 of sales and decline further from there. China could feasibly double its 2030 goal and hit 80% EV penetration, by which point many diesel models are likely to be discontinued.

With these levels of market penetration, in addition to retirements, China’s diesel demand is on track to fall by about 230,000 barrels per day this year, despite increased freight movement. Every additional electric truck that replaces a diesel truck then builds on that reduction. Due to their duty cycle, each electric truck sold has a much greater impact on fuel consumption than each car sold. And the price of fuel combined with the duty cycle has a much greater impact on overall truck economics.

To be clear, these improving results are not due to subsidies, as they appear in the year after subsidies were cut. They are also not due to mandates, as the 2030 goals were met before the ink was dry on their announcement. EV trucks are now more affordable to own and operate than diesel trucks in China. The increase in diesel prices has only increased the cost gap.

Last year, roughly 90% of all electric truck sales globally were in China. That percentage is set to expand this year, despite rising sales in some other countries. Those sales are simply not rising fast enough outside of China to keep pace with China. In comparison, the EU is looking at 2.5% EV market share this year. China is on pace to average roughly as many electric trucks sold per week as Europe sells per year. The US EV truck market looks like a rounding error in comparison at less than one percent.

In addition, China has rapidly been building out heavy truck charging infrastructure as part of the 28 million charging facilities planned to be in operation by the end of 2027, up from 21 million last March. This not only focuses on the major cities but also extends into more rural areas that have been slower to adopt electrification. In addition, several companies are building out battery swap stations. While we have yet to see if battery swap or MW+ charging wins in the truck market, expect the competition between the two approaches to be fierce.

Within Tier 1 cities, I could see a potential phase out of diesel trucks in the near future. Hainan province is already planning to end ICE passenger car sales by 2030, and I expect that to extend to diesel trucks as well. Although rural areas have been slower to adopt, they also tend to be less wealthy and thus more impacted by the changing economics. If diesel prices stay elevated while charging becomes convenient, many rural businesses will not be able to afford to buy new diesel trucks. With the end of diesel truck sales, strict emissions standards and a potential return of scrappage incentives could take remaining diesel vehicles off the road at an accelerated rate.

With fewer diesel trucks on the road in the largest market globally, diesel consumption is set to decline. In addition, the supply chain for diesel parts will also start to decline. As protectionism has targeted electric vehicles, rather than internal combustion  engine (ICE) vehicles, a decline in Chinese (ICE) parts has a potentially significant impact on global markets.

Of course, as EVs take over the majority of heavy truck sales in the fiercely competitive Chinese market, Chinese manufacturers will start looking outside of the country for growth.

The traditional thought would be to look at wealthy Western countries that have substantial buying power. However, many Chinese companies may be a bit reluctant to focus on those markets after the protectionist efforts against electric passenger vehicles. Many European markets, like Germany, also have very high electricity prices, which makes the financial proposition less attractive for commercial vehicles that are heavily motivated by costs. Chinese companies may start focusing elsewhere.

The Middle Corridor could become a focus. This essentially rebuilds the Silk Road land route from China, across the Caspian Sea, on to Turkey. Many of the countries along this route  once had prosperous trade, only to fall into decline when ocean trade routes were established. However, land transport is much easier to electrify than long ocean voyages. China is investing heavily with countries like Kazakhstan along the route with renewable electricity infrastructure. Electrified rail is expanding by 26-fold in Turkey. Electrified trucking is the next logical step, connecting the rail lines to more local production along the route.

Other Belt and Road Initiative (BRI) countries could also benefit. In countries like Ethiopia, which has expensive diesel but electricity prices to businesses of just $0.017/kWh, electric trucks could make perfect sense to transport between new factories and new ports. Trading partners like Vietnam and Thailand could also see an influx.

All this is not to say that local companies will not benefit. Unlike passenger cars, heavy equipment tends to be more modular. Due to the bulk and mass of the vehicles, final assembly is more likely to be localized, with greater use of local suppliers.

Overall, we could see much of the “Rest of the World” (ROW) leapfrog legacy economies in EV truck adoption. The purchase price is no longer prohibitive in China, and the operating costs are much lower. With infrastructure buildout and increasing renewable installed capacity, electric trucking is poised to take over rapidly.

That is not to say that the West will be completely left out. Countries with less prohibitive trade policies toward EVs will likely see increased adoption. While EVs have taken over passenger car sales in Norway, their trucking industry still has a way to go at just 14.4%. And the geographically most eastern country typically lumped in with the “West,” Australia, is likely to see EV truck sales follow a similar pattern to EV car sales. If Canada continues to open up, they could also benefit. Of course, as the majority of the economic benefit from electric trucks comes from their use, inflated trucking costs hurt every company that moves products by truck, and those inflated costs then lead to inflation throughout the economy. That impacts overall affordability. As such, there is a chance that protectionists do not put as much effort into undermining electric truck imports as they put into passenger EVs.

And the EV heavy truck global supply chain will become increasingly well established. This could mean more parts availability for brands outside of China to build competitive vehicles, if those companies have access to global supply chains. Competitive markets will also drive innovation, moving the technology forward at an accelerating rate. Meanwhile the supply chain for diesel trucks will shrink.

While the US still seems to be figuring out how to make a safe EV Semi, battery-powered heavy trucks are becoming increasingly mature products elsewhere. As electric trucks move from majority share to complete market dominance in the largest market, other markets will follow, and the transition will become inevitable. Hopefully, the largest oil producing country in the world will not be left too far behind.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5907. Donated solar project powers community center in unincorporated New Mexico village  Solar Power World’s Projects of Impact

A building that started as a one-room schoolhouse has through the decades expanded to become a lifeline for a New Mexico community far from any city infrastructure, and a recent solar installation has brought new resilience to this place.

Children run outside of Ojo Sarco, New Mexico’s one-room schoolhouse in a photo from January 1943. The structure still stands today as the Ojo Sarco Community Center. Credit: John Collier, Jr./Library of Congress

The structure housing the Ojo Sarco Community Center (OSCC) was founded in the early 20th century. Ojo Sarco is an unincorporated community home to about 300 people that is located about 43 miles northeast from Santa Fe.

“We have no political voice, really,” said Carol Miller, a grant writer and board member for the Ojo Sarco Community Center. “We’re pretty neglected. We’ve lost resources in the past. We were the first wave of people to lose our post office many years ago. Everybody has to commute pretty much for everything.”

That commute is about 40 minutes round trip for basic amenities, she said.

The community center has tried to fill in those resource gaps with services like a twice-monthly food bank, summer meal programs for kids, safety classes and outings for its residents, such as a night at the opera. The OSCC also recently met all the qualifications to be a state library.

PPC Solar, also known as Paradise Power Company, from Taos, built a 7.5-kW solar project paired with 10.6-kWh of LFP energy storage at the OSCC. The contractor built the array at no-cost to the community center. Every year PPC donates a solar array to a regional nonprofit organization, and after three years of applying, the OSCC was awarded the project.

CEO Daniel Weinman’s father founded Paradise Power Company in the 1970s, and the company has installed solar in off-the-grid communities since the start. Its PV donation program is built out of that tradition.

“We’ve always wanted to find a way to give back to the community,” Weinman said. “The best thing that we found — and we’ve been doing it for over a decade now — is doing an annual nonprofit donation system … We’ve had farming organizations, land conservation organizations, and it goes on and on. Really, it’s about finding the right fit. We wanted to make a good impact for this community center, and we felt resiliency was the best way to do that.”

Solar Power World spoke to both PPC Solar and organizers with the OSCC about what effect this solar project has had in the village since it was built in fall 2025.

Larry Miller, project manager and OSCC board member: I think it’s a transformative project for our small community. We like to talk about the history of the community center, and now this is part of it. If someone doesn’t notice the solar panels on the roof, we point it out to them. It’s integrated into our lives here.

Carol Miller: It’s lowered our electric costs, which is really important for us, because we’re such a shoe-string operation. We’ve had some fun talks with the community because they all know we’ll have the charging capacity. We still have internet available, even with the power out. All the kids that come to our library programs, they’re solar oriented. The benefit of this company and its staff just has ripple effects throughout our greater community.

PPC Solar donated a solar plus storage project to the Ojo Sarco Community Center. This array will keep power running in this facility that has become a haven for Ojo Sarco residents. Credit: PPC Solar

Preston Bastardo, sales and design, PPC Solar: The building itself had some challenges with mounting the array on the roof, but that just took some exploration in the attic and making sure that we could land things where we needed to. The building has two large areas, and so fo installation of batteries had some restrictions, there was a good mechanical closet that we were able to use for batteries. Then as far as backup circuits, we did a partial facility backup where we had a critical loads panel next to their main panel. I think everything went pretty smoothly.

Weinman: Well, it really goes back to our roots. In 1972, there was no technology supporting solar at the time. My dad was a pioneer in the industry and he really developed his own controls and safety protections and found used panels and used batteries and was bringing power to places where there was no power. Our house, for example, we started with kerosene lanterns. We didn’t have electricity until we had a solar power system, and it was really life changing.

Being able to build on that legacy and look at our community in that light and be able to offer systems like this to communities that need it — I think that really adds to that legacy and really is profound in a lot of ways. It feels great to be able to do this and be in a position where we can help others realize and experience the benefits of solar power. We want to continue to participate in any way we can.

Carol Miller: We’re a very old community and proud of it. And having worked with local businesses and our electrical co-op, we know it’s really the exception and not the rule because we own our power. Any technology that is shared equally in a low-income community has ripple effects beyond what I think some of us imagined a number of years ago. It’s for everyone.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5905. How Does Oahu Get to an Economically Resilient, 100% Clean Energy Future?

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

#5901. XPENG Sales Soar!

XPENG had a fantabulous sales month in October. We can give you the numbers, but as you can see well enough in the chart above and below, the company’s sales soared to a whole new level last month.

Reaching 50,237 vehicle deliveries, that meant 22% sales growth month over month and 20% sales growth year over year. There were 41,256 XPENG vehicles delivered in September 2026 and 42,013 delivered in October 2025, the company’s previous monthly record. Will it be another year until XPENG beats its monthly sales record again, or is the October 2026 result just the beginning of more record sales in coming months?

Well, there are some reasons to expect even more growth. “In October 2026, the XPENG L05 was officially launched in the Chinese mainland, with deliveries set to begin on November 1, 2026,” XPENG shares. “On October 26, 2026, XPENG obtained a demonstration operation license to conduct Level 4 Robotaxi services in Guangzhou. The approval enables XPENG Robotaxi to advance from technology validation to real-world operational and business model validation, marking a key milestone toward the commercialization of XPENG’s Physical AI technologies.”

A big portion of this sales growth came from tremendous growth in sales outside of XPENG’s home market of China. “Overseas deliveries also reached an all-time monthly high of 13,326 units, up 166% year-over-year,” the company shares.

Overall, things are looking very bright for XPENG. And those EV sales are helping the planet and humanity as well. “XPENG’s electric vehicles delivered from January to October 2026 are expected to reduce life-cycle greenhouse gas emissions by more than 5.77 million tons compared to internal combustion engine vehicles, equivalent to the carbon absorbed by 95.43 million young trees over 10 years,” XPENG wrote in closing, and I’ll close with that highlight as well.

Oh, wait, I’ll also add that XPENG’s big spike in sales in October helped the company to shoot high above similar (but different) rival NIO in their long-term battle as the best selling smart EV startup.

NIO didn’t have its best month, but maybe it will surge again in November and/or December.

#5899. APS expects sales to double by 2041, plans extending coal use to 2038

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Peak demand will grow about 3.4% annually over the next 15 years, driven by industry and data centers, Arizona Public Service said in an integrated resource plan filed Friday with regulators.

APS projects peak demand of more than 14.5 GW in 2041, almost 60% higher than its current record peak of about 9.2 GW, set in August. And the utility expects annual retail sales to grow to about 80,600 GWh in 2041, up from about 34,200 GWh in 2025.

There is some uncertainty behind APS’ load projections, but the utility said it has taken steps to protect customers.

Despite refinements to APS’ large-load interconnection process, the “sustained record levels of interest” from large customers poses “a risk in increased potential for unrealized loads,” APS said in its resource plan, which it files every three years with the Arizona Corporation Commission. To mitigate the risk, APS said it adopted strategies such as having customers commit to “minimum demand and energy-based contract terms, providing collateral in case of default, and instituting early exit fees.”

As a result of population growth and higher levels of economic activity, APS said it expects to add up to 29,000 residential customers annually in the near term, and across the planning period the utility anticipates annual customer growth to be in the range of 1.5% to 2.5% through 2030.

The utility’s preferred plan includes continuing to operate the Four Corners coal plant beyond a planned 2031 retirement. APS said the extension could save customers close to $1 billion.

“The company will continue to evaluate the appropriate time to cease operations between 2031 and 2038, with the decision depending on customer needs, economics and other factors evaluated through future planning processes,” APS said in a statement.

“Reliability is nonnegotiable,” Mike Eugenis, APS director of resource planning, said in a statement.

APS’ planned natural gas resource additions include repowering the Cholla Power Plant as a 387-MW facility by 2030, adding 1,107 MW through new combustion turbines at existing APS sites and uprates to tolling units during 2026-2030, according to the IRP. Under the plan, APS will also add the 1,238 MW Desert Sun combined-cycle facility in 2032 to serve large-customer subscription load.

Customer and clean energy advocates were critical of the APS plan.

APS’ proposal “further delays our move from gas and coal to solar and wind,” Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr said in a statement. “We’re seeing these utilities protect their own bottom lines, instead of making the most economical decisions for ratepayers.”

The utility “should be investing in proven community-based energy solutions, like local solar and battery storage,” Vote Solar West Senior Regulatory Director Kate Bowman said in a statement.

APS “failed to consider how alternative technologies like rooftop solar, battery storage, and other distributed resources could help meet energy needs,” Bowman said. As the ACC considers the plan, regulators will have “an opportunity to push for a more comprehensive approach that puts affordability, reliability, and the interests of customers at the center of long-term planning,” she added.

The utility said it is adding nearly 2 GW of renewable utility-scale solar and 2.5 GW of battery storage in the next two years, “diversifying our resources to keep energy reliable and affordable.” By 2030, APS said it expects to have approximately 6 GW of battery storage on its system.

The utility also expects continued growth in customer-sited resources. APS said behind-the-meter storage adoption is expected to grow every year to 1.2 GW by 2045 — up from 69 MW today. For 2032, the storage forecast is approximately 95% higher compared to the last storage forecast developed in 2023, APS said.

Behind-the-meter solar adoption is expected to slow relative to the 2020-2023 period but “will still experience some modest growth,” reaching 3.7 GW by 2045 — up from 2.1 GW today.

Electric vehicles in APS service territory are expected to grow from 65,000 vehicles today to 1.1 million in 2045, resulting in 5.2 TWh of EV charging load, APS said. In 2042, this forecast is approximately 18% lower compared to the last EV forecast developed in 2023, it noted.

#5898. The industry races into Las Vegas for RE+

The largest tradeshow focused on the U.S. solar, storage and clean energy markets is back in Las Vegas for 2026. RE+ was the 24th largest tradeshow in the United States in 2025 by attendance, and this year’s showing (Nov. 16-19) is expected to be just as large.

The Formula 1 series will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend after RE+ concludes. Photos courtesy: F1 and the Las Vegas Grand Prix

The U.S. solar and storage industries have gotten so big that it appears only the Las Vegas Convention Center can accommodate a tradeshow bringing together nearly 40,000 clean energy professionals in one place. RE+ will be held at the Las Vegas Convention Center each November for the foreseeable future.

The convention center is tucked away from the main strip of casinos, but there is still plenty in the area to see and do. The site is also easily accessible, with a stop on the Las Vegas Monorail.

Conference education programming begins on Monday and sessions continue throughout the show at various stages on the exhibit hall floor. The exhibition opens on Tuesday.

Exhibitors will be spread across three halls in the convention center — the main entrance is at West Hall which extends via an over-street walkway to North Hall, and then Central Hall is accessed beyond a lobby area. There will also be a few outdoor booths to peruse located near food trucks. Visiting outdoor exhibits in November will be more comfortable, as the average high temperature is just 70°F, unlike the late summer heat of September.

Special events at RE+ include a golf tournament on Monday and the Run with the Sun 5K on Tuesday. The “Energy in Motion” block party will be at the Convention Center on Tuesday starting at 7 p.m. in the West Hall.

Formula 1 will race the Las Vegas Grand Prix the weekend following RE+. The course takes drivers down the Las Vegas Strip and around the Sphere, and road closures to the public will begin on Thursday. The Las Vegas Convention Center is several blocks north of the racing circuit, so the conference shouldn’t experience disruptions. After-conference activities might be affected later in the week, and attendees should plan a little extra time getting to the airport if leaving before the weekend.

Solar Power World editors will be covering all the sights at RE+ this year, so be sure to follow along on LinkedIn and right here on the website. But first, here’s a preview of a few must-see booths on the tradeshow floor.

Visit AWM at RE+ to learn about our Strata for BESS cable management solutions. Our Strata Components and Strata System are tailored for leading energy storage applications and engineered for durability and easy installation. Strata supports reliability across the entire lifecycle of stand-alone storage or hybrid renewable power plants.

Construct solar safer, faster, and cheaper. Meet Built’s AI Piling Solution: a fully autonomous piling system for utility-scale solar. Leading contractors are trusting Built to install production-ready piles anywhere, anytime. Head to Central Hall Booth C6310 to learn how to add physical AI to your next solar project.

Hydrelio aiR Optim is our flexible floating racking and anchoring system. It comes from 15+ years of FPV knowledge and expertise, and the first patented and industrialized floating solar solution. It is the result of continuous R&D involvement, innovation, testing and experience building more than 350 FPV arrays worldwide.

IslandDER Meter Socket Adapter (MSA) streamlines solar + storage installs by integrating a microgrid interconnect device (MID), consumption transformers (CTs), voltage sensing and battery communication protocols into a single, compact device that inserts directly into the meter socket in 15 minutes. This eliminates complex electrical work — such as panel upgrades, circuit relocations and gateways — saving installers days of labor on battery backup jobs and preventing unexpected costs for homeowners. Compatible with battery systems from SolarEdge, FranklinWH, Lunar Energy, EcoFlow and Fox ESS, IslandDER is trusted by leading national installers, offering more versatility and compatibility than any meter collar on the market.

Built on gallium nitride (GaN) technology, Enphase’s ninth-generation microinverter platform delivers industry-leading efficiency and cooler operation for residential and commercial systems. IQ9 Microinverters support the latest high-wattage solar panels across single-phase and three-phase applications and seamlessly integrate with IQ Batteries to create a complete AC-coupled solar and storage system.

HD Hyundai Energy Solutions debuts its TOPCon multi-cut module at RE+ 2026, reaching 660 W at 24.4% efficiency. Ultra-precision cutting boosts power density and cell efficiency, backed by the company’s FEOC-compliant, TOPCon-licensed manufacturing capabilities and 20 years of cell-to-module expertise.

Intertek CEA will showcase two new advances in solar quality assurance: AePVI, an aerial inspection service that combines drone and ground-based data with electroluminescence (EL) testing to identify defects across utility-scale projects; and EL-AI, software that reviews the EL image of every module before shipment, moving beyond traditional sample-based inspections.

The OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers and OMCO Choice Fixed-Tilt Mounting Systems support a wide range of solar projects in the utility scale and distributed generation (DG) markets. The OMCO Origin Trackers pair seamlessly with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System that features DC tracker controllers, field-replaceable batteries and a one-mile wireless range for all climates.

The S-5-CP clamp is engineered for mounting solar panels to purlin-framed carport structures. Featuring an optional EPDM gasket for a water-resistant seal, it significantly reduces the risk of leaks while delivering fast, reliable installation. Designed for durable, high-efficiency solar carport applications, it pairs seamlessly with the S-5! PVKIT for rail-less installations.

Built around Siemens’ U.S.-manufactured 375-kW 3TL500 string inverters, WATTBLOC Virtual Central Station (VCS) transforms utility-scale solar through a modular, scalable power-block architecture. Leveraging standardized power-block designs up to 5 MWAC, its distributed architecture improves availability, simplifies maintenance, accelerates deployment, lowers lifecycle costs and supports expansion across utility-scale solar portfolios.

SiteCaptureAI’s contextual AI automates jobsite documentation, quality control and reporting across the full solar project lifecycle, from surveys and installations to O&M and TPO financing. It uses photo and video intelligence to verify field reporting, flag compliance issues and cut QA/QC work, reducing truck rolls and financing rejections.

Sungrow North America will highlight three systems: the PowerTitan 3.0, packing 6.9 MWh into a 20-ft container with a 12,500-cycle life; the PowerStack 255CS, which arrives pre-installed and pre-commissioned; and the SG4400 modular inverter, built from independent power modules that keep a single fault from taking the full unit offline.

As solar matures, installers need new ways to create value beyond installation. At RE+, Tigo will highlight repowering aging systems and adding storage with virtual power plant participation. The booth will feature inverter and battery solutions, repowering resources and VPP capabilities that extend system life and create new revenue opportunities.

Trinasolar and Trina Storage will showcase the highly durable Vertex N Shield PV module, certified to withstand 55-mm direct hail impact and 75-mm impact at a 45° stow angle, the Vertex S+ residential module, with enhanced protections against environmental risk, and the next-gen Elementa + Electra battery energy storage solution (BESS).

Weidmuller’s Weather Stations and String Monitored DC Combiner Boxes deliver comprehensive monitoring for utility-scale solar plants. Weather Stations provide accurate environmental data, cybersecurity, edge processing and flexible sensor integration. String Monitored Combiner Boxes enable real-time string-level performance monitoring, early fault detection, reduced maintenance effort and improved plant ROI and LCOE.

#5894. Exelon, Invenergy complaints at FERC seek capacity market changes

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Exelon asked the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission to order PJM to adopt a “prompt” capacity auction; Invenergy wants the Midcontinent grid operator to let HVDC lines receive capacity payments.

Exelon and Invenergy Transmission this week filed separate complaints at the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission seeking changes to the capacity markets run by the PJM Interconnection and the Midcontinent Independent System Operator.

Exelon asked FERC to order PJM to shift to a “prompt” auction from its current forward capacity auction schedule, according to a Tuesday filing at the agency. Currently, PJM holds annual base capacity auctions to secure power supplies three years in advance.

Under Exelon's proposal, PJM would hold its capacity auctions two months before the delivery year begins, starting with the 2030/31 delivery year. If FERC approves the proposal, PJM would hold its next capacity auction in December for the 2029/30 delivery year as planned, and then it would hold its next auction in early 2030.

Pushing out the date of the 2030/31 auction would give PJM time to overhaul its capacity market, which Exelon contends is no longer working properly, according to the complaint.

Adopting a prompt auction would help ensure that auctions are based on more accurate load forecasts, resource availability data and capacity accreditation levels, Exelon said. It would also facilitate more efficient resource entry and exit through improved market signals, the Chicago-based utility company said. Exelon’s utilities have about 10.7 million customers in PJM’s Mid-Atlantic and Midwest footprint.

Exelon asked FERC to make a decision on the complaint by Jan. 31, the last day before PJM is set to release the planning parameters for the 2030/31 delivery year base auction, which is set to be held in May.

PJM is reviewing the complaint and will reply at “the appropriate time,” Jeffrey Shields, a PJM spokesman, said.

The grid operator is accepting proposals from stakeholders on how it could reform its capacity market through its Power Reliability Through Market Design initiative, Shields noted. PJM has received more than 30 responses to its request for papers on the issue. They will be discussed at a Nov. 13 workshop.

When it kicked off the effort in May, PJM proposed three broad reform pathways: stabilizing its existing capacity market, rationing grid reliability among customer classes or regions and shifting to a market focused on energy and ancillary services and away from the capacity market.

Meanwhile, Invenergy Transmission on Wednesday told FERC that MISO improperly bars high-voltage, direct-current merchant transmission lines from its capacity market.

MISO counts the capacity value of HVDC lines when it decides how much capacity it needs to buy in its capacity auctions, a move that reduces the amount of capacity it acquires, according to the complaint.

But, MISO’s rules prevent those lines from being accredited as capacity resources or their owners otherwise being paid for their capacity value, allowing the grid operator to benefit from the lines for free, Invenergy said.

“MISO cannot rely on that capacity, to the benefit of other customers, while refusing to provide any avenue for the entity that provides that capacity to receive commensurate compensation,” the Chicago-based company said.

Instead of recovering their costs through FERC-approved cost-of-service rates, the owners of merchant lines recover their costs via rates negotiated with their customers, Invenergy noted.

“Interregional merchant HVDC transmission lines only receive compensation if there is a product or service — such as capacity — for which they can transact with potential customers,” Invenergy said.

Without capacity payments from MISO, “there is no product or service that can be the basis of a merchant-based transaction in MISO,” Invenergy said.

Using a “direct loss of load” accreditation methodology, Invenergy’s Grain Belt Express HVDC transmission line could provide more than 1,800 MW of accredited capacity, according to the complaint.

The 5-GW, 600-kV Grain Belt project is slated to run about 800 miles from Kansas to Indiana. Invenergy plans to build the roughly $11 billion project in two phases, with the first phase running from southwest Kansas to central Missouri.

#5890. Entergy sees progress on Arkansas data center deal, strong Q3 sales

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The New Orleans-based company separately inked firm pipeline contracts for enough natural gas to support 10 new combined-cycle power plants in Louisiana, CEO Drew Marsh said Monday.

Entergy’s amended application to serve a controversial Google data center will save its Arkansas ratepayers at least $500 million more than the $1.6 billion previously agreed, CEO Drew Marsh told investors Monday during the company’s third-quarter earnings call.

The New Orleans-based utility holding company saw earnings increase more than 20% over the year-ago period helped by warmer-than-average weather across its four-state U.S. territory in the Midcontinent Independent System Operator’s southern zones, he said. Total electricity sales rose 5% year over year.

Entergy made progress on two key regulatory priorities in Louisiana, which accounts for nearly half of the holding company’s retail sales.

State regulators in October approved Entergy Louisiana’s $1.2 billion, 500-kV Babel to Webre transmission project, Marsh said. And Louisiana Public Service Commission staff separately reached a settlement with the utility that Entergy said “resolves all outstanding issues” between the company, LPSC staff and one intervenor in a contested proceeding related to several gigawatts of generation and related infrastructure to serve Meta’s planned Hyperion data center campus.

The LPSC is set to vote on the matter next month, Entergy said.

Meanwhile, Entergy’s Mississippi utility asked state regulators last month for state clearance to issue bonds that would offset approximately $237.8 million of costs related to Winter Storm Fern, which Entergy said caused “severe damage” to its infrastructure.

And in Texas, Entergy said it has energized the Orange County Advanced Power Station, a 1,215-MW combined-cycle gas plant built to support load growth in the state’s southeastern corner.

Entergy filed the amended Google service contract with Arkansas regulators last month. If approved, Entergy would add 350 MW of battery energy storage at two sites and procure 440 MW of solar from Pattern Energy’s nearby Big Island Solar project, the company said in a required securities filing this week.

The Arkansas Public Service Commission is set to hear the matter in January, Entergy said.

The amended contract would expand power demand from Google’s West Memphis data center, but the exact amount and other key details are redacted, the Arkansas Democrat-Gazette reported on Oct 20.

On Monday, Marsh said Google would pay $600 million toward the cost of the battery systems and suggested the tech giant would avoid passing costs to other Entergy ratepayers.

“This agreement ensures that Google will cover its fair share, which includes the cost of the new investments as well as their share of system costs,” Marsh said.

Entergy Arkansas is one of five utility companies owned by Entergy, and earned about 48% of its sales from industrial customers in 2025. The parent company’s four-state territory has been a popular destination for large-scale data centers like Google’s Arkansas project and Meta’s even larger Louisiana campus.

Together, data center and traditional industrial customers account for a large share of Entergy’s expected future sales growth, much of which is not yet reflected in the company’s near-term capital plan, Marsh said.

“We have a strong pipeline of projects in varying stages that are not in our base plan,” he said. “Data center interest remains strong and in the range of 7 [GW] to 12 GW. Our traditional industrial segments continue with discussions for 3 [GW] to 5 GW.”

Those large-load pipeline figures are largely unchanged from recent quarters’ projections. But the company’s projected four-year capital spending has risen significantly over the past year, to about $55 billion as of Sept. 30.

Marsh said the Google contract allows Entergy to keep base rates flat for Arkansas customers. However, that’s not the case in Louisiana, where Meta “is helping [to] offset some [infrastructure] costs, but not necessarily to make it go back to flat like we were in Arkansas,” he said.

To serve the Meta data center, Entergy plans to spend about $12.9 billion to build seven combined-cycle gas-fired power plants with 5,278 MW of combined capacity, plus a $1.4 billion transmission line and 200 MW of battery storage. It has also asked Louisiana regulators to approve a corporate sustainability agreement that would see Meta — doing business as Evest LLC — cover the cost of 2,500 MW of new renewables and energy storage resources.

To support the new gas generation, Entergy inked firm transportation agreements with “large midstream and pipeline companies” for 1.325 billion cubic feet/day of natural gas, Marsh said.

“That's enough for Entergy to supply fuel to 10 new combined cycles in Louisiana … and also helps build new pipeline capacity that benefits our industrial customers' growth,” he said.

#5888. Sierra Club Response to Midterm Election Results

Democrats must seize on this opportunity to take ambitious action for the climate and our democracy.

Washington, DC — While votes are still being counted, election officials have called enough races that it’s clear that Democrats have retaken the US House of Representatives and are expected to retake the Senate.

Sierra Club-endorsed candidates including Annie Andrews (SC), Deb Haaland (NM), Keisha Lance Bottoms (GA), Manny Rutinel (CO), Gina Hinojosa (TX), and more won their races with commitments to fight for clean air, clean water, and a clean energy economy.

In response, Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“Voters across the country have made a resounding call for new leadership that will prioritize healthy and affordable communities, uphold our democracy and constitutional rights, and hold polluters, utility companies, and the Trump administration accountable. This is a clear rejection of Donald Trump and Congressional Republicans’ war on affordable clean energy and their politics of corruption and corporate greed that have left Americans facing skyrocketing energy bills and polluted communities while fossil fuel CEOs reap the profits.

“Sierra Club members and supporters across the country organized, phonebanked, and knocked doors for these candidates up and down the ticket, and now we will work to make sure they seize on this opportunity to take ambitious action for the climate and our democracy. They must hold the Trump administration accountable for its corruption and unlawful attempts to sacrifice the health and safety of our communities, and invest in a clean energy future for all of us.”

In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5885. Co-located solar, storage jumps to 16% of all solar installations, up from 2% in 2017: EIA

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The share of solar projects co-located with storage is projected to pass 30% over the next two years, based on planned additions, according to a report from the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

The share of new solar projects deployed with co-located battery storage this year is 16% — or about 41 GW — as of September, and may reach 19% by year’s end, said an Oct. 30 report from the U.S. Energy Information Administration. It is likely to pass 30% in the next two years, the agency estimated.

The trend has increased significantly over the last decade, as only around 2% of projects, or 5 GW, of new solar capacity was co-located with storage in 2017. In 2023, co-location jumped to 25% of projects, then fell over 2024 and 2025, according to EIA’s data — but is projected to pass 30% over the next two years, based on planned additions.

“When operators co-locate battery storage with solar, they can direct solar electricity output to the grid or to the battery, depending on the wholesale prices,” EIA’s report said. “Wholesale prices in regions with large solar participation tend to fall when solar contributions are high and rise as they decrease.”

“Plants with both solar and battery storage can manage the combined output of their generating resources to maximize their value in a changing wholesale price environment,” EIA added.

An Oct. 22 report from the Solar Energy Industries Association found that from 2021 to 2026, “solar generation increased 421% in Texas and 90% in California, while battery storage capacity grew more than 26-fold in Texas and nearly sevenfold in California.”

When comparing the same June to August period, SEIA found that “CAISO used 2.5% more power than the same time in 2021 with an average wholesale electricity price 40% lower after accounting for inflation. ERCOT used 30.1% more power than the same time in 2021 with an average wholesale price 42% lower after accounting for inflation.”

EIA’s report noted that Southwest states “have higher-than-average solar capacity shares relative to their total generating capacity and also have higher levels of co-located utility-scale solar and battery capacity.”

“As of September, 13% of the total U.S. utility-scale generating capacity is solar photovoltaic, and 18% of that is co-located with battery storage,” EIA said. “The share of solar generation co-located with battery capacity in California, Arizona, New Mexico, and Nevada is more than double the national average.”

A September analysis from Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory found that at the end of 2025, there were 436 co-located solar-plus-storage projects in the United States, with a total of 29 GW of solar capacity and close to 17 GW of storage capacity.

Within the interconnection queue, LBNL found that solar-plus-storage facilities comprise 86% of proposed hybrid facilities, at close to 361 GW of planned capacity.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

The PJM Interconnection’s plan for curtailing large loads that don’t have matching new generation intrudes on state jurisdiction and appears to be discriminatory, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission said.

Shortages of skilled labor and equipment, as well as local opposition, are threatening even high-profile projects backed by big tech companies. Developers are responding by embracing flexibility and alternative technologies once considered too unwieldy.

#5881. Eaton CEO: AC to DC migration for data centers ‘is real’

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The electrical equipment giant says it’s poised to capitalize on the trend by investing in solid-state transformers. But the “timing of that transition is not under our control,” said CEO Paul Ruiz.

The CEO of one of the world’s biggest suppliers of electrical equipment sees the AI-driven global construction boom continuing for years — and driving a shift from traditional low-voltage systems to 800-volt direct-current architecture for data centers.

In August 2025, Eaton Corp. paid $86 million for Resilient Power Systems, a solid-state transformer developer.

Using magnets and semiconductors rather than specialized steel alloys and vast amounts of copper wiring, solid-state transformers, or SSTs, are more compact and efficient than traditional low- and medium-voltage transformers. Data centers built on the 800-volt direct-current architecture that Eaton expects will soon become the industry standard could reduce non-IT capital expenditures by up to 18%, according to a recent McKinsey analysis.

Earlier this year, the Electric Power Research Institute, New York Power Authority and North Carolina State University tested a 1-MW SST under “real-world” conditions and cut power losses in half compared to a traditional transformer configuration.

Paulo Ruiz, CEO of Eaton, told investors on Tuesday that the company’s 15-kV, 2-MW SST became, “to our knowledge,” the first multi-megawatt unit to pass all tests under a new UL Solutions standard for the technology.

However, it may be some time before Eaton SSTs are widely deployed, he cautioned.

“The good thing for us to keep in mind is that this direction of travel, in terms of migrating from AC to DC, is real because of the large power savings that the customers see in this new technology,” he said. “But the timing of that transition is not under our control.”

He added that SSTs are only one of several “key building blocks” needed, along with DC breaker, power quality and advanced cooling technologies.

“The AC architectures are not bad, and we are fine either way,” Ruiz said. “We win in either path.”

Ruiz said he sees no letup in the global demand for power equipment from data centers and utilities.

Eaton, which is headquartered in Ireland but is one of the largest suppliers in the U.S. market, makes and markets low- and medium-voltage electrical products such as single- and three-phase power quality systems, utility-grade power distribution equipment, circuit protection and power reliability equipment. It reported a record $8.9 billion in third-quarter sales, up 28% year over year.

“We track the [worldwide] projects that are announced, and now they amount to 366 GW,”  Ruiz said, of data centers. “Contrast that with the installed capacity of 50 GW. It's an enormous build-out. In my opinion, this will last for many years to come.”

Eaton predicts end market growth of about 11% in 2027. In 2025, data centers and distributed IT accounted for 21% of sales, while utilities accounted for 11%.

A little less than half the total of third-quarter sales came from Eaton’s Electrical Americas segment. That unit benefits from broad-based demand from data centers and other electrification “mega-projects,” helping to widen profit margins, Ruiz said.

The Electrical Americas segment saw its backlog rise to $16 billion – up 33% year over year –  while orders increased 39% on a rolling 12-month basis.

Ruiz emphasized that data centers were not Eaton’s only high-growth customer segment. Utility investment and other large-load industrial customers are propelling business, too, he said.

“Demand continues to run ahead of what we can convert,” Ruiz said. “This is a business that continues to benefit from powerful secular trends, utility investment, grid modernization, industrial reshoring, AI-driven data center expansion and broader electrification.”

In the past two years, Eaton has acquired multiple companies with data center exposure.

The most recent was a roughly $920 million deal for COL Group, an Italian manufacturer of medium-voltage electrical distribution equipment, grid automation technologies and modular power systems that Eaton said would boost its sales to data center and utility customers in Europe.

In March, Eaton paid about $9.6 billion to acquire Boyd Thermal, “a U.S.-based global leader in thermal components, systems, and ruggedized solutions for data center, aerospace and other end-markets,” the company said in a securities filing this week.

The Boyd pickup will help Eaton develop integrated power and cooling architectures for data center customers and “deliver differentiated grid-to-chip solutions while increasing our exposure to some of the fastest-growing segments of the data center market,” Ruiz said.

#5879. “War” Secretary Pete Hegseth Endorses 15 Minute City Model

You just can’t make this stuff up. While conservatives around the world are waging holy war against the “15 Minute City” concept, US Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth has gone all-in on the idea. His newly announced “Fortress America” military resilience plan includes a new vision for military bases that embraces walkability and quality of life. If that sounds like the 15 Minute City plan in action, it is. Oh, lordy…

Walkable communities are as old as the hills. That’s what people did before automobiles hit the scene and cities were organized around cars. Credit for applying the walkability model to 21st century urban planning goes to the Franco-Colombian scientist Carlos Moreno, who formalized the 15 Minute City concept in 2016. The idea got a further boost of attention when the Mayor of Paris, Anne Hidalgo, pitched walkable, bike-able neighborhoods as part of her successful re-election campaign in 2020.

The COVID-19 lockdown of 2020 was also a pivotal moment, with a surge in work-from-home households drawing attention to the need for local resources as a matter of survival and resiliency in case of a widespread emergency.

The 15 Minute City model covers a lot of ground, but the basic idea is that people should be able to do their shopping, recreation, socializing, and other essential routines within walking or biking distance from their homes, making cars largely optional within the neighborhood. Walkable access to schools and health care is also part of the plan.

The argument for 15-minute cities (also called 15-minute neighborhoods or 15-minute communities) is also pretty straightforward. Walking is healthier than sitting in a car. Avoiding injury or death from car accidents is another plus. Walking also reduces carbon emissions, and it saves money on gas.

In addition, walkability levels the playing field, so to speak. Everyone in the 15-minute zone has walkable access to basic resources, regardless of whether or not they can afford a car. In other words, there is a significant equity and inclusion element to the concept…

…and, that’s where the trouble kicks in. In 2022 the World Economic Forum posted an article by Lisa Chamberlain, the founder of the urban communications and business firm Strategies for City Builders. Chamberlain describes a 15 Minute City as characterized by diversified, mixed-use neighborhoods built to withstand shocks like COVID-19 and climate change.

“As climate change and global conflict cause shocks and stresses at faster intervals and increasing severity, the 15-minute city will become even more critical,” Chamberlain emphasized, with a further emphasis on equity and inclusion:

“Specifically, communities that foster and maintain social and economic relationships don’t have to be wealthy, but they do need to be walkable and safe, with both residential and commercial buildings intact. And, I would add, for 15-Minute Cities to thrive, not just survive crises—and this cannot be stressed enough—they must also have plenty of mixed-income and equitable housing, as well as digital access.”

It didn’t take long for the conspiracy machine to kick into action around the globe. In February of 2023, for example, the Australian news organization ABC radio summed up the 15 Minute City conspiracy theory thusly:

“To some, it’s an urban planning concept that promotes sustainable and healthy living.

“To a small group of others, it’s a plot by ‘tyrannical bureaucrats’ to take our cars and control our lives, which could lead to a real-life Hunger Games scenario.”

“Limiting cars and promoting pedestrian or bike access is framed as a slippery slope to government-run, open-air prisons,” ABC continued.

Yikes! ABC also cites the Canadian psychologist Jordan Peterson. who railed against “idiot tyrannical bureaucrats” who “decide by fiat where you’re ‘allowed’ to drive…and, make no mistake, it’s part of a well-documented plan” (emphasis added).

Ohfergawdsakes, of course there is a well documented plan, and it’s called urban planning. And, that’s where the US Department of Defense comes in.

On September, in a move that sailed under the CleanTechnica radar, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth announced the new “FORTRESS America” plan, with FORTRESS representing “Federal Operations for Resilience, Troop Resources, Energy, Supply, and Survivability.”

Stripping away Secretary Hegseth’s testosterone-fueled purple prose in the September 30 announcement, FORTRESS AMERICA applies the 15 Minute City urban planning model to major Defense Department bases in the US, ensuring they can operate independently of civilian infrastructure for a sustained period of time. That includes everything needed to support community life in addition to the supplies needed to sustain military operations.

FORTRESS America even goes beyond the initial 15 Minute City concept. As envisioned under the “Next Great American Base” element of the program, a central feature of the plan consists of access to a reliable source of on-site energy.

Don’t just take my word for it. “The program aligns basing and infrastructure projects to ensure they are collectively advancing toward greater independence,” Hegseth stated in the official order, with independence referring to the capability to sustain operations within a facility that is cut off from civilian resources. In other words, sustainable urban planning for military facilities.

Hegseth tasked the Under Secretary of War for Acquisition and Sustainment to carry out the new order. The base design part of the order was passed along to Dale Marks, the Assistant Secretary for Energy, Installations, and Environment.

Marks did not let the 15 Minute City grass grow under his feet. In a public presentation on October 29, he emphasized the importance of building quality of life into a sustainable model for military facilities in the US.

“Let us build installations worthy of the warriors who call them home,” Marks emphasized. “The nation shouldn’t expect warfighters to focus on high stakes, multidomain operations when they come home to base housing with black mold, failing plumbing and leaky roofs.”

“Substandard living conditions are not minor inconveniences. They are an active drain on morale, combat readiness and our retention of top-tier talent,” Marks elaborated.

It’s not just a matter of rehabbing military housing. In the presentation, Marks made the case for a full blown 15 Minute City model. “The Next Great American Base will include a compact, walkable concept with premier schools, advanced healthcare and town centers that foster community,” Marks continued, neatly summing up the fundamentals of 15 Minute Cities in a nutshell.

Marks anticipates that state governors and other stakeholders will compete to see where the first “Next Great American Base” will be constructed. Once complete, the new facility, and its 15 Minute City, will serve as a model for future projects.

As for the on-site power generation angle, nuclear energy gets the pole position — for now. As Marks and Hegseth both emphasize, though, speed is of the essence. Results are demanded within months, if not weeks. That leaves plenty of room for solar-enabled microgrids to throw their hat into the FORTRESS America ring. After all, if speed-to-power is the name, solar and batteries are the game…

Image: The 15 Minute City model calls for walkable, bike-able access to basic services and resources, a concept that has been embraced by none other than the US Department of Defense (courtesy of Rutgers University).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.

#5878. Participation in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico VPP program doubled in 2026

Sunrun announced that participation in its Puerto Rico distributed power plant has doubled to more than 60,000 customers, representing nearly 70,000 home batteries. The distributed power plant frequently and reliably delivers 45 MW of dispatchable power for four hours at a time, providing critical support to Puerto Rico’s electric grid.

Since April, Sunrun has dispatched its power plant more than 60 times to weather severe energy shortfalls and prevent rolling blackouts. During an extended period of unseasonably hot temperatures this fall, Sunrun dispatched its home batteries to the grid for 18 consecutive days beginning October 13.

Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant has scaled rapidly since 2024, when approximately 4,000 customers were enrolled. Enrollment surged to more than 30,000 customers in 2025. Sunrun is the largest aggregator in Puerto Rico’s Customer Battery Energy Sharing program.

Puerto Rico’s electric utility operator, LUMA, said in June and again in July that energy from home batteries helped “maintain system stability and prevent outages that would have affected thousands of customers.”

“What began in Puerto Rico out of necessity for family resilience has become a vital blueprint for the U.S. mainland, where severe weather and the proliferation of AI data centers are demanding new energy solutions,” said Sunrun CEO Mary Powell. “Sunrun is uniquely positioned to provide these solutions, whether to support a grid at its breaking point or to provide capacity for hyperscalers looking to come online.”

Sunrun’s increased dispatching capacity in Puerto Rico comes after residents on the island experienced an average of 36 hours of power outages in 2025 that were not caused by major adverse weather, which is 19% more than the previous year, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration. When severe weather is factored in, residents experienced an additional 23 hours of power interruptions.

Customers who participate in Sunrun’s Puerto Rico distributed power plant choose how much energy to share, with most contributing a small portion of their total battery capacity. This approach ensures they retain reserve power for personal backup power in the event of grid outages. Participants are financially compensated for the power they choose to share, while Sunrun generates recurring revenue by operating the distributed power plant.

#5877. DTE expands investment plan 30%, will sell unregulated subsidiary to fund growth

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

After the $3 billion sale of DTE Vantage, DTE “becomes a higher growth premium utility” and “a simpler business,” CEO Joi Harris told analysts Thursday.

DTE Energy has agreed to sell its non-utility subsidiary for $3 billion, allowing the company to sharpen its focus on its regulated electric utility and fund a growing investment pipeline, company officials said Thursday.

DTE serves 2.3 million customers in southeastern Michigan, including Detroit.

DTE Vantage builds and operates energy projects including renewable natural gas. The sale to institutional investors is expected to close in the first half of 2027, DTE President and CEO Joi Harris said on the company’s third-quarter earnings call.

Data center growth is providing “once-in-a-generation opportunities” and “real affordability benefits to our customers,” Harris told analysts.

“Selling Vantage just allows us to sharpen our focus on the regulated utility, and it helps us execute on those investments, and it reduces clearly our equity and debt needs to fund them,” she said.

After the sale of Vantage, DTE Energy “becomes a higher growth premium utility” with long-term operating earnings per share growth of more than 9% annually after 2027, and “a simpler business construct,” Harris noted.

DTE’s new 5-year investment plan, spanning 2027-2031, now stands at $44.2 billion, including $39 billion at its DTE Electric subsidiary and $5.2 billion in its gas operations. The 2026-2030 investment plans totaled $30 billion and $4.5 billion, respectively.

On the electric side, the company’s spending plan includes $23 billion for cleaner generation and $12 billion in distribution investments to harden DTE’s grid system and improve reliability. Generation investments include 7.6 GW of solar and wind additions over the 5-year plan, 2.1 GW of natural gas and 2.7 GW of energy storage.

DTE received approval from Michigan regulators in October to supply a 1-GW Google data center, and last year finalized a 1.4-GW deal with Oracle.

“These projects will provide unprecedented customer bill benefits by spreading system fixed costs across a larger base of electric sales to help keep bills more affordable for existing customers,” Harris said. “The timing of these customer load ramps also creates an opportunity to delay our next electric rate case filing.”

DTE filed a $474 million electric rate case with the Michigan Public Service Commission in April. If the utility receives regulatory approval, Harris said the company won’t need to request new rates again until 2029.

Harris said the company is “continuing to have discussions” with regulators about multiyear rate making “in anticipation” of the state legislature taking that up toward the end of 2027.

“We have a strong and have maintained a strong relationship with both sides of the aisles over our history, and we are expecting nothing different with this cycle,” she said following a national surge for Democrats in the midterm elections, driven partly by backlash to data centers and energy costs.

DTE continues to see “strong demand” for new large-load development, Harris noted. Beyond the Oracle and Google data center deals, the utility currently has a pipeline of 5-6 GW of additional opportunities, including roughly 2 GW in advanced discussions, she noted.

“We are encouraged by the continued level of customer interest and remain focused on securing an additional agreement by year-end,” she said.

#5874. Nabrawind’s Crane-Free Wind Towers Face a Supplier Test

Spain’s Nabrawind has developed a way to install exceptionally tall wind turbine towers without bringing an enormous crawler crane to the site. Its Nabralift system uses a tubular upper tower supported by a three-legged steel structure. Hydraulic equipment raises the assembled turbine while modular sections are inserted below it near ground level. The lifting frame can then be removed and used on another installation. This addresses genuine logistical and construction problems, particularly at remote sites where specialist cranes are expensive to transport and where wind can interrupt conventional lifting operations.

The company has progressed beyond demonstration-scale hardware. In September 2026, UL Solutions issued type certification for Envision’s 7.8 MW turbine on Nabrawind’s 188-meter tower. Fortescue is using 17 of those tower configurations at the 133 MW Nullagine wind project in Western Australia’s Pilbara region, where construction is underway. Nabrawind previously erected a 160-meter prototype in Spain in under two weeks and has supplied smaller commercial towers in Morocco and France.

The engineering benefits are credible. Nabrawind reports using roughly 20% less tower steel than comparable conventional tubular designs and reducing foundation concrete by as much as 80%, depending on the design and site. Those are supplier claims rather than independently established savings across all wind farms. The potential to avoid large cranes, simplify transport and raise turbines in windier conditions could also matter substantially to total installed cost and construction schedules. High hub heights only earn their keep when those costs are justified by the electricity generated over the project’s life.

The current deployment has an unusual commercial structure. Fortescue acquired Nabrawind in September 2025 and is building Nullagine to serve its own mining operations. Fortescue has the capital, internal electricity demand and industrial incentive to support a technology it owns. Successful installation in that context would be important evidence of construction performance, but it would not establish that an unrelated wind developer can finance and maintain the same system under ordinary third-party commercial arrangements.

Nabralift’s proprietary construction approach creates a procurement issue. Conventional tubular towers can be sourced from several qualified manufacturers, and experienced contractors can compete for much of their construction work. A developer selecting Nabralift currently depends on one supplier for the core tower system, specialist lifting knowledge and associated support. Wind turbine original-equipment manufacturers also create supplier dependencies, but concentrating another critical part of the project in a proprietary installation system requires its own contract and risk assessment.

A type certificate provides valuable technical assurance about the certified turbine-and-tower configuration. A lender or insurer still needs enforceable delivery schedules, long-term warranties, repair and maintenance provisions, clarity on replacement parts, and a workable remedy if the supplier’s capacity or commercial priorities change. These questions become more important when a wind farm must operate for 25 years or longer and there is no qualified alternative provider of the same system.

Nabrawind’s next important proof point is an independently developed and financed large wind farm that accepts those risks on commercial terms. Qualified installation partners, credible long-term service arrangements and greater competitive capacity could make its engineering advantages easier for developers to buy. The 188-meter tower has earned technical attention; its broader market depends on financing and procurement confidence catching up with the machinery.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Nabrawind’s self-erecting design, Fortescue’s Nullagine deployment and the supplier concentration that independent wind developers would have to manage. It follows my earlier assessment of 300-meter wind towers, where construction economics were the central challenge.

#5873. Eversource’s financial risk eases as Revolution Wind hits full commercial operations: CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Eversource Energy’s third-quarter income fell 22% year over year, to $346 million, driven in part by a lack of income from Aquarion, a water utility subsidiary the company sold in June.

The 704-MW Revolution Wind wind farm off the coast of southern New England is fully operational, delivering needed power supplies to the region and “significantly” reducing Eversource Energy’s risk of additional financial obligations for the project, Joseph Nolan, Eversource chairman, president and CEO, said Thursday during a third-quarter earnings conference call.

“This significant milestone comes at a critical time for our region, where extreme temperatures have underscored how important it is to bring every potential energy source online for customers,” Nolan said.

Two stop-work orders issued by the Trump administration delayed the project, which is being built by Ørsted.

Eversource in 2024 sold its 50% stakes in three offshore wind farm projects, including its share of the Revolution Wind project. As of June, the utility company had set aside about $409 million for potential obligations related to the two wind farms sold to Global Infrastructure Partners, according to a U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing.

Eversource expects to close out any contractual obligations with GIP next year, a process that will “take some time to fully reconcile,” Nolan said. “Our current assessment is that we have an adequate reserve on our books to cover this potential obligation.”

Eversource’s Connecticut Light & Power is working with state regulators to review its proposed $451 million rate hike proposal, Nolan noted during the call.

“We believe the constructive engagement we've had with regulators and stakeholders has created a path forward that balances affordability with the investments needed to deliver safe, reliable and resilient service,” he said.

CP&L is proposing a multi-year, performance-based rate framework that includes gradual, annual inflation index rate changes to avoid significant rate hikes, according to John Moreira, Eversource CFO. Eversource expects the Connecticut Public Utilities Regulatory Authority will issue a decision on the rate case in June, he said.

Under the rate proposal, CL&P would spend $1 billion installing advanced meters in Connecticut, Nolan noted.

Also, ISO New England has selected a $2.2 billion joint transmission proposal from Eversource and Avangrid to bolster the grid in the Boston area, Nolan said. Eversource has a $700 million share of the project.

The metering installations and the transmission project aren’t included in Eversource’s current capital expenditure plan, Nolan noted.

Meanwhile, Moreira said Eversource expects that a federal appeals court could issue a decision by the early fall next year on a challenge seeking to reverse the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s decision to cut the return on equity earned by New England’s transmission owners.

In March, FERC ruled that the base ROE for New England’s transmission owners had been set too high and retroactively lowered it, triggering refunds to customers going back as far as 2011. Eversource and Avangrid have said their utilities owe about $880 million and $203 million in potential refunds.

New England’s transmission owners are working with ISO-NE and load serving entities to prepare the refund, according to Moreira.

“This is an incredibly complex labor-intensive process to recalculate 10-plus years' worth of load settlement and billing data,” he said. “Given these complexities and the significance of this refund process, we know it will be challenging to process these refunds by FERC's deadline of May of next year.”

#5871. PowerBank completes 3-MW New York community solar project

Renewable energy developer PowerBank has completed the 3.26-MW Camillus community solar project in Camillus, New York, and has received permission to operate. Solar Advocate Development, LLC, the project’s owner and EPC, built the array on a capped landfill, which it acquired from PowerBank in spring 2025 in a $7.3 million transaction.

The project will soon accept community solar subscriptions. Solar Simplified will manage customer acquisition and subscription services for this community solar project.
