#5869. Data centers don’t need special treatment to get power faster

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Projects that make their demand more predictable, controllable and financially secure should have a clearer path to service, writes Seyed Madaeni, CEO and co-founder of Verse.

Seyed Madaeni is CEO and co-founder of Verse, an energy software company specializing in battery dispatch optimization for data centers.

Even the best-funded project can’t operate without electricity, and the grid often can’t deliver hundreds of new megawatts as quickly as AI developers need them. Access to power has become a competitive advantage, and companies are searching for ways to get it faster.

Today, developers see two obvious options. They can wait years for traditional grid upgrades, or they can build private power plants onsite. The first can move too slowly. The second brings fuel-supply, equipment, permitting and community challenges of its own.

A third path to data center interconnection is taking shape. Across the country, utilities and grid operators are creating clearer ways for large loads that can reduce the risks they bring to the system. Depending on the market, a data center might guarantee a minimum payment, agree to reduce its grid demand when the system is strained, provide better operating data or contract for new generation and storage.

These policies differ, but they share one idea: If a data center wants faster or more certain access to power, it should give the grid greater certainty in return.

That is a better approach than advancing projects mainly because they promise jobs or investment. Projects that make their demand more predictable, controllable and financially secure should have a clearer path to service.

Utilities often need to invest in substations, transmission equipment and other infrastructure years before a data center reaches full operation. They are building against forecasts that can change, and their queues may include projects that never get built.

Another utility might choose a different percentage or structure. But a company asking the grid to reserve an extraordinary amount of power should be willing to stand behind that request financially. This protects existing customers from paying for infrastructure built around demand that does not materialize. It also helps serious projects distinguish themselves from speculative ones.

Other markets are focused on how data centers behave once they connect.

A large facility can draw hundreds of megawatts, change its consumption quickly or disconnect during a grid disturbance. At that scale, it’s part of how the power system operates, whether it considers itself a grid participant or not.

Texas is responding with clearer technical and operating expectations. A facility that meets the Electric Reliability Council of Texas’ ride-through requirements clears one important gate on the path to energization, alongside required modeling, stability and interconnection reviews. Through controllable-load and bring-your-own-generation pathways in ERCOT's new Batch Zero process, developers can elect dispatchable curtailment under ERCOT’s real-time control in exchange for earlier energization.

Meaning, a data center must be able to reduce what it takes from the grid when the system is strained. It could curtail consumption, switch to onsite resources or coordinate several assets. What matters is that the response is available when promised.

Southwest Power Pool is making a similar trade through its High Impact Large Load framework. Developers that accept real-time forecast reporting, ramp-rate limits and remote-disconnect capability can have their interconnection study completed within 90 days, clearing the way for an agreement soon afterward.

In both markets, developers give the grid better visibility and more control during constrained periods. In return, they receive a faster and more predictable process.

PJM Interconnection is approaching the challenge through resource adequacy that prioritizes whether the region has enough capacity to meet demand when the system is under the most stress.

Under PJM’s proposed Interim Resource Adequacy Service framework, certain new large loads could connect even when the region doesn’t have enough capacity to serve all forecast demand at its traditional reliability standard. Loads that didn't bring qualifying new capacity or receive an applicable capacity allocation could have been required to reduce their grid consumption during limited emergency conditions. FERC rejected most of the framework in October on jurisdictional grounds, but it encouraged states to decide which retail customers are curtailed when the system is stressed, which keeps the same trade on the table.

A data center could reduce that exposure by bringing qualifying generation, storage or demand-side resources. Otherwise, it would accept service that is less firm than what existing customers receive.

Bringing new capacity does not make transmission and distribution constraints disappear. A project may still need local upgrades, engineering studies and regulatory approvals. But if the system is short on capacity, a new load should either help fill the gap or be prepared to reduce its demand when the shortage becomes critical.

Different rules, one underlying question.

Each market is responding to its own constraints, but the question at the heart of these efforts is the same: What can a data center guarantee in exchange for access to power?

Federal regulators are now pushing that question across the country. In June, FERC directed all six regional grid operators under its jurisdiction to justify their existing large load tariffs or propose reforms. States are moving in parallel through large load tariffs and other requirements intended to ensure that data centers pay their share and manage their impact on the grid.

Today, developers learn the answer after spending time and money evaluating a site. Requirements can differ significantly from one utility or region to the next, making it difficult to compare locations, price the obligations attached to service and design the right energy infrastructure from the beginning.

Utilities and grid operators can make this process clearer. A developer should understand early on how specific commitments affect access to power.

The answer isn’t one national tariff or one required technology. Every grid has different needs, and data centers can provide flexibility through batteries, onsite generation, workload management or a combination of resources.

Utilities and grid operators should define a clear menu of performance-based pathways. Developers should know what they can commit, what the grid will require and what faster or more certain service they can receive in return.

Clearer options will help utilities separate real projects from speculative requests, allow developers to understand the cost of speed before committing to a site and give regulators a stronger basis for determining whether an arrangement protects reliability and existing customers.

The fastest data centers will not necessarily be the ones that find a way around the grid. They will be the ones that give the grid enough confidence to move forward with them.

Washington, DC — As Democrats regain control of the US House of Representatives and the US Senate, the Sierra Club today released a new legislative policy blueprint for the incoming Congress that will deliver for the American people by addressing the affordability crisis, holding polluters accountable, preserving our public lands, and protecting public health and safety.

“Voters have sent a clear message that they reject Trump’s war on clean energy and the destructive and corrupt agenda of the administration and its allies in Congress. The people have spoken, and the new Congress should waste no time in responding by fighting to reverse the damage Trump has caused these past two years, and leading our country to a stronger, more resilient future,” said Sierra Club Legislative Director Melinda Pierce. “This blueprint guides the way to lower costs, stronger infrastructure, and healthier communities — all wildly popular goals that the new Congress must get serious in pursuing right away.”

#5866. Sempra expects 20% increase to Oncor’s spending plan, driven by load growth

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“It’s too soon to tell exactly” how the results of the midterm elections will impact Oncor, said CEO Allen Nye. “We continue to believe that both parties want a strong and growing Texas.”

Oncor’s base capital plan of $47.5 billion for the next five years is expected to increase by 20% in its upcoming annual update, driven by “continued strong customer and load growth from a variety of sectors, all over our system,” said Oncor CEO Allen Nye during a Thursday third-quarter earnings call from the company’s majority owner, Sempra.

Sempra CEO and chairman Jeffrey Martin said during the call that Oncor — a utility with more than 14 million customers in Texas — continues to see “remarkably strong demand growth, having recently reached a new peak on its system of 33 GW, a 6% increase over the prior record set last year.”

“This increase underscores the robust economic growth that's occurring in Texas,” Martin said. The state’s growth is “broad-based,” he said, and “data centers are obviously the lion's share of it, but ... there is a significant amount of non-data center growth.”

In response to an analyst question about the potential impact to state data center growth and Oncor itself due to the results of the midterm elections, Martin said, “We look forward to working with the new administration in Texas.”

“It's too soon to tell exactly what it all means,” Nye said. “But generally, the way I think about it, we provide an essential service that's needed regardless of the party in power … We continue to believe that both parties want a strong and growing Texas.”

When it came to state headwinds, Martin voiced concern about California, where Sempra subsidiaries San Diego Gas & Electric and Southern California Gas Co. face financial risk as a result of ongoing uncertainty in the state regarding wildfire liability.

The California legislature's failure to pass a bill in its last session to reduce the effects of wildfire liabilities on SDG&E and other utilities is a “significant adverse credit development,” Fitch Ratings said in a September report.

As Pacific Gas and Electric Co. and Southern California Edison's CEOs did during their own third-quarter earnings calls, Martin stressed the need for the California legislature to pass legislation on wildfire liability reform. The credit downgrades will “constrain future utility service,” he said, and the situation “clearly underscores that inaction doesn't work, and that there's a real need for a timely solution.”

In its third-quarter earnings filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Sempra noted that the scope of potential damages caused by the January 2025 Eaton fire “could materially reduce or exhaust the Wildfire Fund. Edison stated that it is currently unable to reasonably estimate a range of potential losses associated with the Eaton fire.”

“Accordingly, SDG&E is unable to estimate a range of potential loss resulting from any reduction in available coverage from the Wildfire Fund,” the filing continued.

Martin also noted that the California Public Utilities Commission last week issued a proposed decision denying Sempra’s petition for modification of a 2024 general rate case decision, in which Sempra alleges that CPUC made an error in not including an associated revenue requirement.

Sempra aims to have CPUC correct the error so that its California utilities can collect $188 million in associated revenue in 2027, said CFO Karen Sedgwick during the earnings call.

“This is just a 2027 issue because we have a new [general rate case] starting rates in January of 2028, so that won't carry over,” Sedgwick said. “It's a one-year issue. We think it's a mistake. We think it gets fixed. If for any reason it couldn't, we're going to try to mitigate that.”

Martin said the CPUC’s decision was “proposed … It's not final. There's a series of regulatory avenues that we will appeal to try to get the right answer.”

#5865. Bay Area Air District Moves Forward with Nation’s Strongest Water Heater Standards

Health advocates, environmental groups, heat pump installers, local electeds celebrate decision as.

SAN FRANCISCO — The Bay Area Air District voted today to move forward with the strongest clean appliance standards in the nation, putting millions of Bay Area homes on a path to switch from gas water heaters to zero-emission alternatives. Beginning in 2028, households will be required to install water heater appliances that meet clean air standards as old gas units burn out, with exemptions approved today for low-income households and complicated installations.

Gas appliances in Bay Area homes and buildings generate more smog-forming nitrogen oxide pollution than all of the region’s passenger cars combined. The Air District estimates its clean appliance standards for water heaters and furnaces could prevent up to 85 premature deaths and 15,000 asthma attacks each year.

The switch also saves families money. New research from RMI finds $360 in annual savings for Bay Area residents from switching from gas water heater to a heat pump. Analysis from SPUR projects $315 million in rebates for home energy efficiency will be available in the next three years to support the transition.

The vote follows thousands of public comments in support of the standards, despite an industry-backed opposition campaign linked to oil interests.

Supporters of the standards released the following statements:

“The research makes it clear that climate policies and affordability go hand-in-hand and today the Bay Area is taking the historic step of making clean home appliances the standard for a better future,” said Melissa Yu, Campaign Strategist for Clean Heat, Sierra Club. “A transition toward zero-emission gas heaters will reduce a key source of smog pollution, launch programs to help get more people access better technological solutions and lower energy costs. Today’s vote is a victory for cleaner air and for energy affordability.”

“Today’s vote will supercharge the job growth we’re already seeing at heat pump businesses like mine, opening new pathways for early-career Bay Area workers looking for family-sustaining wages and meaningful work. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership in approving these standards. We’re proud to be on the front lines of equipping homes with energy-efficient technologies that cut energy bills, clean the air, and make homes more comfortable,” said Owen Krebs Grimsich, CEO and owner of 1-888 Heat Pumps.

“Today, the Bay Area chose clean air over fossil fuel pollution. Under these standards, when a household’s gas water heater wears out, it will be replaced with cleaner, more efficient technology like a heat pump, so millions of residents will breathe easier and save money on their energy bills. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership, and the rest of the country should follow its lead,” said Tony Sirna, Deputy Policy Director for Buildings at Evergreen Action.

“Today’s vote is a big win for clean air, lower utility bills, and climate leadership,” said Berkeley City Council member Igor Tregub. “The Bay Area is moving forward with the nation’s strongest water heater standards, and millions of families here will breathe cleaner air as a result. These standards build in the flexibility we need to make the transition workable, while growing the market for clean technologies that lower bills and create good jobs.”

“Today, the Bay Area Air District said yes to saving lives and no to gas industry attempts to slow progress to clean our air,” said Fernando Gaytan, senior attorney on Earthjustice’s Right To Zero campaign. “The Bay Area is now one step closer to making non-polluting appliances the  norm thanks to the leadership of board members and staff. This policy will show the rest of the country what’s possible.”

“Today’s vote is a step forward for cleaner air, healthier homes, and a more affordable energy future. By advancing these standards, the Air District shows we can move away from polluting gas appliances without leaving families behind,” said Sam Fishman, sustainability and resilience policy manager, SPUR. “Bay Area cities, utilities, and regional programs are taking the important steps to invest more than $120 million annually in home electrification and energy-efficiency incentives. With these tools, we can make our homes safer for our planet and our health, too.”

“As regional leaders in building electrification, Acterra sees this vote as a vital win for local communities and public health,” said Lauren Weston, Executive Director at Acterra: Action for a Healthy Planet. “Transitioning to zero-emission heat pump water heaters clears toxic nitrogen oxide pollution from our neighborhoods while lowering utility bills and accelerating equitable access to clean, energy-efficient technology for all Bay Area households.”

“The Air District Board’s vote today to support the implementation of Rule 9-6 is a watershed victory for healthy and safe communities, moving from our dependence on fossil fuels to a cleaner, greener source of energy,” said Leslie Alden, Executive Director of Act Now Bay Area. “The Air District staff, the experts, the working groups, and so many dedicated members representing the Bay Area worked tirelessly from 2023 show once again that the Bay Area leads the way.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5864. ARRAY Technologies and Shoals plan to merge companies

Solar tracker and racking manufacturer ARRAY Technologies and electrical balance of systems (eBOS) provider Shoals Technologies have initiated a stock-for-stock merger to combine both companies.

“This transaction represents a compelling opportunity to create meaningful value for customers of both ARRAY and Shoals by bringing together our complementary strengths. Although structural and electrical systems must operate seamlessly together in the field, they have historically been designed and sourced through separate channels. This combination changes that. Together, we expect to deliver a differentiated, technically integrated and interoperable platform that simplifies project execution, enhances project economics and accelerates innovation for customers,” said Kevin Hostetler, CEO of ARRAY.

According to the press announcement, ARRAY will be Shoals’ parent company after the transaction, with Shoal’s CEO Brandon Moss assuming the role as CEO for the overall company. ARRAY’s CEO Kevin Hostetler will transition to a special advisor role for the company.

“Today marks an important milestone for Shoals, ARRAY and the customers we serve. By bringing together two highly complementary businesses with experienced teams, we are creating an integrated platform with broader capabilities, deeper customer relationships and a stronger foundation for long-term growth. We believe the combined company will be positioned to better service customers, deliver more innovative and integrated solutions, realize meaningful synergies and pursue expansion opportunities across the solar value chain, data center markets and beyond,” said Brandon Moss, CEO of Shoals.

The companies plan to create packaged warranties across their structural and eBOS products. As manufacturers, both ARRAY and Shoals have supplied their products to solar and energy storage projects globally.

ARRAY is known for producing single-axis solar trackers that use an inter-row rotating mechanism. Over the years, the company has iterated on its trackers to use multi- and dual-row architectures, and to be deployed on undulating topographies. These rotating solar structures are supported with proprietary tracker operations software with considerations such as shading and hail and wind stowing.

Shoals manufactures combiner boxes and cable management solutions for multi-market scale solar and energy storage projects, as well as data centers.

“Having had the privilege of serving as chairman of both ARRAY and Shoals, I have seen firsthand the complementary products, talented teams and shared commitment to customers offered by both companies,” said Brad Forth, chairman of the board of directors of ARRAY and Shoals. “Together, ARRAY and Shoals are well positioned to deliver enhanced value to customers, shareholders and employees. I also want to thank Kevin for his leadership and important contributions to ARRAY, as well as his valuable insights as he transitions to a Special Advisor role. I look forward to continuing to work with Brandon, who brings proven leadership, deep industry expertise and strategic vision to lead the combined company in this next phase of growth.”

Both ARRAY’s and Shoals’ boards have reportedly unanimously approved the merger, with Forth abstaining from the vote. The merger is expected to complete in Q1 2027.

While not mergers, ARRAY Technologies has been acquiring companies producing technologies complementary to solar trackers. Last year, ARRAY acquired fixed-tilt racking and foundations manufacturer APA Solar Racking, and just acquired Affordable Wire Management, a cable management solutions provider, this past September.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5863. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5862. Duke Energy to stay course on data center-fueled growth plan after ‘historic’ election

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company’s large-load pipeline of 15.4 GW has remained virtually unchanged since the first quarter of 2026 as it pursues a $103 billion spending plan heavily focused on new natural gas generation.

Despite a “historic” election night in which large swaths of the public voted for candidates promising to rein in data centers, Duke Energy's vision for the growth of the company remains unchanged, CEO Harry Sideris said during a third quarter earnings call Thursday.

The company’s large-load pipeline of 15.4 GW has remained virtually unchanged since the first quarter of 2026 as it pursues a $103 billion spending plan, heavily focused on natural gas, to meet demand.

“We have a track record of ... working with both sides of the aisle,” Sideris said. “We don't see any changes in that focus or any changes in our ability to work with those that were recently elected.”

He said his company shares elected officials’ goals of ensuring reliable, affordable service and “making investments that help grow the state” and create jobs.

“They share our goal, too, of protecting our customers and making sure these large loads pay for their fair share,” he added.

The company said its 15.4 GW large-load pipeline includes 7.8 GW of signed electric service agreements — up 200 MW from the first quarter of the year.

In response to an analyst question about what it would take to get the remaining 7.6 GW over the finish line, Sideris said the company was operating in a “dynamic environment.”

“They're going very well, but it's taking longer because of the complexities,” he said.

By 2031, Duke plans to construct 4.8 GW of battery storage and more than 10 GW of new natural gas generation, about 5 GW of which is already under construction in North Carolina and Indiana. The company has secured about one-third of the $10 billion in equity it plans to issue to support the plan, CFO Brian Savoy said.

Turbines and gas supply for the new projects are already under contract, and the company is exploring pairing a 2.8 GW gas generation project in North Carolina with a liquefied natural gas storage facility that Sideris said would “provide greater supply flexibility and diversity while reducing exposure to price volatility.”

“Our resource plan makes clear the important role natural gas will play in the near term to ensure reliability,” he said.

The gas-focused expansion plan has already met some resistance in North Carolina, where regulators denied an application for a Certificate of Public Convenience and Necessity to build a 255 MW gas generating unit citing its “staggering” cost and uncertain need.

“We still feel very confident that that's a good project,” Sideris said. “It's a small project, but it is a low-cost solution to help the generation need that we have in the state to serve our customers reliably and affordably.”

Savoy said the company is currently spending about $1.5 billion per month on planned projects, and plans to step up the pace of spending to $2 billion per month beginning next year.

“The capital plan is moving up in a sizable way,” Savoy said.

Third quarter electric utility earnings grew $159 million year-over-year thanks to warm weather, a 2% increase in retail electric sales and a number of newly finalized rate cases and riders. Settlements under consideration in North Carolina would raise rates at Duke Energy Carolinas and Duke Energy Progress, respectively, by $496 million and $338 million over two years, while hearings are set for January for a $90 million requested increase in Ohio and an initial multi-year rate plan will be filed in Indiana later this year. The company plans to keep rates in Florida flat in 2027.

The company also continues to advance a slate of large-load tariff proposals, including in North Carolina, Florida, Indiana and Ohio. A settlement in North Carolina could result in the approval of a large-load tariff in that state by the middle of this month, according to company documents.

#5861. Who Controls the Battery in Your Driveway?

Tesla brought home power backup to America. In Australia, an update is switching it off. North American owners just got Powershare with Powerwall. Australian owners of existing Teslas got a software update that appears to block the third-party gear they use to power their homes. It is a test case for who really controls the battery in your driveway.

Mark Purcell is an electrical engineer and energy advocate based in Noosa Heads, Queensland, Australia. He owns two Teslas and a bidirectional DC charger, and has been a member of the Australian Electric Vehicle Association since 2022. He has contributed content to CleanTechnica previously.

On 12 October, my Model Y ran my house. Through a Sigenergy bidirectional DC charger in my garage on Australia’s Sunshine Coast, it supplied about 25 kW to my home. The battery in my driveway holds several times the energy of a typical home battery, and I have already paid for it.

That may not last. Owners in several countries report that Tesla software from the 2026.38 branch, rolling out since October, cuts third-party discharge within minutes. On the Tesla Motors Club forum, owners describe power stopping after about three minutes with a battery management alert, or after 15 to 20 minutes whatever the load. The old Camp Mode and Service Mode workarounds no longer help. Drive Tesla Canada has covered the reports. EleJoy, Tlyard and Sigenergy users are all affected, and Tlyard now warns customers that Tesla’s 2026 software detects “simulated charging” and may cut the power link.

The release notes? “Security improvements” and minor fixes. Nothing about switching off a function owners use every day.

Here is the part that should interest readers everywhere. The same 2026.38 branch delivered Powershare with Powerwall home backup to owners in the US and Canada. That is a good thing. Tesla has finally joined Ford, GM, and Kia in letting the car back up the house.

Australia did not get it. Tesla Australia has said Powershare cannot be retrofitted to existing vehicles, and here Powershare is vehicle-to-load only. So, for every existing Tesla on Australian roads, third-party equipment is the only way to put the car to work for the home. If the software now blocks that equipment, Australian owners are left with nothing: not Tesla’s solution, and not anyone else’s.

The Tesla Australia owner’s manual says: “Do not use the Battery as a stationary power source. Doing so voids the warranty.” Tesla’s own Australian warranty page is narrower. It excludes damage caused by third-party adaptors or chargers, not the whole warranty.

Australian law is narrower again. Under the Australian Consumer Law, statutory consumer guarantees cannot be signed away by a line in a manual. Apple found this out when the Federal Court ordered A$9 million in penalties over the “Error 53” affair, after Apple told customers that third-party repairs had cost them their rights. American readers will recognise the shape of this argument from the right-to-repair fight.

Australia is a world leader in rooftop solar and is now subsidising home batteries at scale. The next step is obvious: the biggest battery most households will ever own is already parked outside. Bidirectional charging is how that capacity reaches the home and, in time, the grid.

That whole idea depends on interoperability. If one carmaker can switch off discharge overnight, through an over-the-air update the owner cannot roll back, and without saying so, then every bidirectional charger is a bet on that carmaker’s goodwill. Chargers bought in good faith become stranded. Competing suppliers lose access to the fleet. Grid operators lose flexibility they are counting on.

The ACCC, Australia’s competition and consumer regulator, has already named “denying interoperability” as conduct that can distort competition in digital platform markets. A car that decides which charger it will talk to is a platform in all but name.

I am deferring Tesla updates on both my cars, so I am also missing whatever genuine fixes they contain. I have asked Tesla in writing to hold updates until it confirms discharge will keep working. I have lodged a complaint with the ACCC under Australia’s competition and consumer laws. I have also asked the Australian Electric Vehicle Association, the national voice of EV users, to back it.

Tesla may have a real safety or engineering reason. If so, it should say what it is, and show why a less restrictive answer would not work: certified partner equipment, discharge limits, or a published interface that charger makers can build to.

Three things, and they apply well beyond Australia. Carmakers should disclose functional changes in plain language before they ship. Owners should be free to use the bidirectional charger of their choice. And regulators should set interoperability rules for vehicle discharge, as many already do for solar inverters. If we can make inverters speak a common language, we can stop cars locking out the chargers they plug into.

My car still powers my house. It does so only because I have stopped it updating. No owner, in any country, should have to choose between a current car and a working home battery.

“I am looking forward to the discussions around this topic,” David Waterworth, who helped get this article published, adds.

We publish a number of guest posts from experts in a large variety of fields. This is our contributor account for those special people, organizations, agencies, and companies.

#5859. Elon Musk Threw Away $474 Million (Or More) … But May Benefit From Failing

Some people think Elon Musk has lost his mind. Others think he’s always been a charlatan and has just had an insane amount of luck. Some people think he’s a genius and that everything he touches will turn to gold. And then there’s also a decent portion of folks who think he’s an absolute idiot about some things but brilliant — or at least very clever — when it comes to others. Whatever you think of Elon Musk, though, there’s no denying he just threw away about half a billion dollars.

Musk reportedly spent at least $474 million on the midterm elections (because one individual is allowed to do that, apparently), and yet his picks — the key people he supported — got completely crushed. The politicians he spent all of that money to get elected did not get elected, and were actually not even close to getting elected. Apparently, voters in their jurisdictions thought they were horrible choices, sometimes historically so. Imagine if all of that money he spent had been spent on something good and useful instead! (Perhaps saving the lives of starving, poverty-stricken children and babies around the world? Perhaps stopping the spread of horrible diseases, since the US government has dropped such efforts on several threats? Perhaps to provide subsidies for electric vehicles, like Tesla sells, since Republicans in Congress and Donald Trump killed those? Perhaps to use solar power instead of fossil fuels to power AI data centers?)

Why did Elon Musk spend a whopping half a billion dollars (or more) on the 2026 midterm elections?

I know there are some people who don’t see this, but it’s been clear for a while that the following things are true about Elon Musk:

Musk largely targeted Texas with his political spending, and Texas had an epic, historic blue wave. Something that has been dreamt about for decades came true in dramatic fashion despite Elon Musk spending hundreds of millions of dollars to try to stop it. Ironically, Democratic politicians are the ones who support electric vehicles and solar power, key segments of Musk’s empire. It was California, led by Democrats, that birthed Tesla Motors and enabled it to grow and develop into an industrial behemoth. It was US policies under Democratic presidents and Congresses that also provided critical help for years upon years. So, despite Elon Musk’s worst instincts and biggest efforts politically, will his businesses actually do better under the newly elected Democratic leadership? Perhaps there are some corporate issues that need reigned in or need better regulation, and perhaps people Musk brazenly and often quite rudely campaigned against will squeeze down extra hard on his corporate actions, but policymakers who support cleantech over fossil fuels should, overall, mean more good news than bad news for Tesla and its broader mission, right?

Sure, Musk threw away $474 million and lost bigly, but is he going to be better off from failing in his efforts to pick the people running his current home state and representing it in the US government? What are the actual risks he and his companies face from the people he heavily campaigned against winning their elections? What are the benefits he and Tesla will get from fossil-loving, pollution-peddling Republican politicians no longer running the show in Texas and in Congress? One has to wonder.

By the way, one of the key people Musk campaigned against, James Talarico, has a message specifically for him. “Texas is not for sale,” Talarico said. Musk spent at least $150 million to try to get Talarico’s opponent elected. “The people of Texas delivered a crystal-clear message to the world’s first trillionaire: Texas is not for sale,” Talarico added. “The power in this state, in this country, belongs to the people, and we’ve got to make sure it stays that way.” What do the people want? Well, the people want clean air, clean and cheap energy, and less money in politics. Shouldn’t all of those things help Musk? On that last one, after all, if he wasn’t allowed to pour so much money into politics, he wouldn’t have thrown away half a billion dollars.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

#5858. Infant Bleeding Rises as Newborn Vitamin K Shots Have Fallen

— Brain bleed risk more than doubled in infants who missed routine vitamin K shots

Newborns who missed routine vitamin K shots had higher bleeding risks and more than double the risk of intracranial hemorrhage, a nationwide study of over 5 million infants showed.

Infants who did not receive prophylactic vitamin K shots, the share of which increased from 3.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2025, had a 47% higher bleeding risk by 6 months (288.0 vs 185.9 per 10,000; adjusted risk ratio [RR] 1.47, 95% CI 1.43-1.51), reported Heather Burris, MD, MPH, of the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, and colleagues in JAMA.

They also had more than twice the risks of intracranial bleeding (33.0 vs 13.2 per 10,000; adjusted RR 2.43, 95% CI 2.26-2.62) and death with concurrent bleeding during the birth hospitalization (2.9 vs 1.1 per 10,000; adjusted RR 2.69, 95% CI 2.09-3.47).

Diagnosed vitamin K deficiency tripled in infants and vitamin K deficiency bleeding more than quadrupled during this period, noted Elizabeth Wolf, MD, MPH, of Virginia Commonwealth University in Richmond, and co-authors in a related JAMA Pediatrics research letter.

Among children ages 0 to 6 months, the rate of vitamin K deficiency climbed from 11.8 to 37.3 per 100,000 from 2017 to 2025, while vitamin K deficiency bleeding rose from 24.0 to 107.4 per 100,000.

Brain hemorrhages occurred in 6.9% of infants diagnosed with vitamin K deficiency and 2.4% of those with vitamin K-deficient bleeding.

Both studies leveraged electronic health record data from January 2017 through December 2025 using the Epic Cosmos research platform to evaluate infants born at 35 weeks' gestation or later. The JAMA study followed 5.24 million newborns for the first 6 months of life. The JAMA Pediatrics study evaluated 11 million U.S. infants ages 0 to 6 months who received care from 2017 through 2025.

Vitamin K is required to activate clotting factors II, VII, IX, and X, Wolf and co-authors noted. Newborns can be deficient in vitamin K due to low transplacental transfer, limited gut synthesis, low breast milk concentration, and poor gastrointestinal absorption, they said.

The American Academy of Pediatrics has recommended vitamin K shots since 1961. However, parental rejection of prophylactic vitamin K has increased in recent years, placing more infants at risk for vitamin K deficiency bleeding, Wolf and colleagues observed.

"This study shows the real-world consequences of vitamin K refusal," Wolf told MedPage Today. "Vitamin K-deficient bleeding can be very serious in babies. When the bleeding occurs in the brain, it can result in permanent neurologic damage or even death."

Classical vitamin K deficiency occurs between 2 days and 1 week after birth and leads to umbilical bleeding and bruising. Late vitamin K deficiency occurs between ages 1 week and 6 months and has a 30% to 60% risk of intracranial hemorrhage. Studies have shown that infants without vitamin K shots were 81 times more likely to develop late vitamin K deficiency bleeding.

Temporal trends in the JAMA study showed year-over-year increases in the number of infants with bleeding and these trends differed significantly by vitamin K receipt, noted Laura Kair, MD, MAS, of the University of California Davis School of Medicine in Sacramento, and colleagues in an accompanying editorial.

"While the causes underlying these trends remain uncertain, the authors speculate that maternal-infant dyads may have had more severe medical comorbidities in recent years, and they emphasize that bleeding rates rose significantly faster among those with vitamin K nonreceipt," Kair and colleagues wrote.

"As with all serious but rare outcomes, vitamin K deficiency bleeding risk is a difficult concept to communicate, and when presented as a percentage may seem purely theoretical," the editorialists noted.

"Based on the authors' calculation of number needed to harm, one additional infant would have a bleeding event if just 114 infants did not receive vitamin K," Kair and co-authors pointed out. "This number needed to harm provides a powerful narrative that may be more readily accessible to parents, supporting more effective counseling and decision-making."

Study limitations included reliance on Epic Cosmos data, which excludes infants treated at facilities using other electronic health record systems.

"In countries such as France, Germany, the Netherlands, Switzerland, and Canada, oral vitamin K is offered either routinely or alternatively to intramuscular vitamin K," wrote Wolf and colleagues. "Although the oral form can be less effective in preventing late vitamin K deficiency, a harm-reduction approach that offers oral vitamin K as a second-line alternative may be warranted in the U.S. if rejection of injectable vitamin K continues."

#5857. Latin America EV Sales Report: With Over 200,000 Units Sold in Q3, EVs Now Stand at 12% Market Share!

Through 2026, EVs have gained significant momentum all over the world (safe for the US), and Latin America has been no exception. Sales grew by almost 140% in the third quarter, and clock in at a very healthy 120% growth through 2026, bringing the yearly total to more than half a million units. If this trend continues, it’s all but certain we will surpass 700,000 units by the end of the year, and perhaps even get to 800,000.

Another interesting bit of trivia is that sales reached 220,000 units in the third quarter, not too far behind the USA’s 266,000. In theory, this means it should be possible for LatAm to surpass total US EV sales as early as Q1 2027 … though, I would be much happier if the USA’s market starts growing strongly again and thus Latin America cannot get ahead.

Now, to be fair, the US is much more BEV-heavy (242,000 units sold, vs 127,000 in LatAm), whereas Latin America prefers PHEVs to a higher degree (93,000 units sold vs 44,000 in the US), but, still, these are impressive results for a region overall much poorer than the US, and with a vehicle market around a third of the size. For over a decade, it was the richest countries in the world that led EV adoption, but — as many of us predicted on this very site — that has now changed.

As mentioned earlier, total EV sales reached 220,000 units, surpassing for the first time the 200,000 mark and growing a healthy 140% YoY, and a strong 17% from Q2. BEVs grew faster than PHEVs (146% vs 130%), and thus now represent 57.5% of all EV sales in the region, slightly below the 58% result for Q2.

Market share grew to 12%, up from 10.7% in Q2 and 5.5% in Q3 last year, a pretty strong result. This brought market share above 10% through 2026, 5.9% BEV and 4.3% PHEV.

Latin America’s overall vehicle market is growing at rates of around 10% annually, but thanks to strong EV growth, combustion-based powertrains (HEV, MHEV and ICEV) are stagnant: they fell by 3.7% last quarter, and barely grew by 3.1% this one, meaning it’s very likely that we’re reaching “Peak Combustion” in Latin America and the market will only go down from 2027 onwards.

Sales wise, we have a pretty stable list, with Brazil being the unsurprising winner, followed by Mexico and Colombia, just as in Q2. Uruguay follows, but then we have a new rising star: Ecuador, which nearly reached 5,000 BEVs sold last quarter! Costa Rica, Chile, and Argentina close the top 8 and make up all the countries with over 1.000 quarterly BEV sales.

PHEV sales are much more varied, though. Brazil, of course, leads again, but following come Mexico and Argentina, with Colombia in 4th place and Ecuador in 5th.

Market share: again, we have a pretty constant list, with the first 8 positions unchanged. Uruguay, unsurprisingly, leads the region with a very strong 50.7% EV market share (45.7% BEV), followed by Costa Rica, Colombia, Ecuador, and Brazil. Notable mention to Paraguay, the sixth country in our region to reach 10% EV market share through a quarter, but in this case thanks to PHEVs.

This has been an eventful year due to Donald’s dubious defense decisions, but even as high oil prices have been driving massive EV adoption and/or massive ICEV crashes all over the world, I feel the situation in Latin America isn’t as clear cut.

In the US and Europe, fuel prices have gone up by around 40–50%, at least in the case of gasoline. Now, prices have gone up similarly in some countries in Latin America, including Panama, Paraguay and Perú, but in many others, like Brazil, Colombia and Mexico, it has barely moved, either because of significant subsidies — direct or indirect — to maintain prices, or because it was already expensive before the war and the government did not make any adjustments to its price.

One would think that the countries most affected by high fuel prices would be at the forefront of the EV transition, but no: the largest increases in EV sales only partially track gasoline prices. For example: Panama and Perú are still lagging behind the region despite having the highest increase in the region (>50% in both cases), whereas Colombia and Brazil are sprinting ahead despite relatively stable fuel prices. What gives?

The answer, I believe, is linked to EV prices and how they compare with their ICEV competition. Yes, I know the TCO is a very important metric, but evidence in our markets point to the sticker price being the main driver of adoption. The cases of Brazil and Colombia on one hand, and Peru and Panama on the other, exemplify this very well:

Brazil and Colombia have had pretty stable fuel prices, up around 10% or less since the war started, yet both have booming markets, up 160% and 190%, respectively, with EV market share getting close to 20%. Meanwhile, Panama and Peru have seen gasoline prices spike over 50% from their pre-war costs, yet the former only showed a meager 57% growth in September, and the latter, though clocking in at a more impressive 270% YoY, remains at a disappointing 1.3% market share.

The main difference has to do with the nature of the market. In Brazil and Colombia, combustion vehicles are relatively expensive — in no small part due to tariffs to protect local industry — and EVs have effectively reached price parity with their combustion counterparts. Meanwhile, in Panama and Per,u EVs are just as cheap if not cheaper than in Brazil or Colombia, but combustion cars are significantly cheaper, with some entry-level models at or below USD$10.,00. This makes customers hesitant to try the new technologies by the simple logic that even if the EV will save a lot of money in the long run, $6,000 will buy a lot of gas.

And in Peru, this is particularly relevant insofar as gas-mobiles from Chinese brands are very popular. Changan, Jetour, Geely, and JAC together account for 20% of the Peruvian market largely thanks to their hyper-affordable combustion cars, and since the Chinese gas-mobile market is collapsing, it’s likely these brands would rather keep it like this for a few more months so they can move some of the inventory they won’t be able to sell at home before betting it all on EVs.

In general, this tracks for most markets, where EV prices compared to traditional combustion cars are probably the most relevant factor to keep track of. And of course, fuel prices do have an influence, but it seems it’s rather limited compared to, for example, what we’re seeing in Europe.

But the lessons are clear: Want to improve EV sales? Either make them cheaper, or make gas-mobiles more expensive.

At the beginning of this year, I was hopeful we would see 10% market share in the fourth quarter, normally the highest in the year. We went past that in Q2, and at 12%, we’re sitting far beyond what I considered feasible only 10 months ago.

Yet, there’s still a long path ahead. Aside from the 5 leading markets, we’re yet to see a country getting consistent BEV market share above 5%, and several large markets — Chile, Argentina, and Mexico — are still lagging. PHEVs have raised the bar in several countries, Paraguay most important of all, but we would rather see more BEVs.

This is not to be overtly pessimistic. The results so far are great, much better than we would’ve expected even a year ago. But as things improve, the bar rises, and I’m now waiting to see the moment when we will see 20% market share (Q3 2027?) or when e-truck sales will start to ramp up (2027? 2028?).

Passionate for all things Latin American, I’ve been closely following the energy and mobility transitions since they started to become present south of the equator.

#5856. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5855. The US Midterm Election Results Remind Us To Focus On The “Foundation Of Our Democracy”

The country feels different as the US midterm election results come rolling in. When all of the votes are counted, Democrats are set to win the national popular vote in all House races by about 13 percentage points. The voters have spoken in huge numbers: they want leaders who make everyday life a little bit easier.

Now the politicians must answer, and, essentially, they need to design policies that lower costs for ordinary people in the US. It’s not about revenge: revenge is too distinct from US consumers’ lived experiences.

Paul Krugman, the Nobel Prize-winning economist, says that one of the main things “Democrats need to do now is hold not just Trump’s officials, but also his enablers, accountable.” Representative Robert Garcia of California posted on social media in the same vein: “Lawyer Up” to the MAGA losers.

No, that’s playground antics. Instead, the next two years must demonstrate a willingness to listen to US citizens as each family struggles to make ends meet with groceries, household utilities, healthcare, and other daily costs.

Roy Cooper’s Senate campaign in North Carolina had a mantra that permeated every stop on his statewide tour: Make Stuff Cost Less.

James Talarico, Jon Ossoff, Roy Cooper, among others, grounded their campaigns in economic populist agendas. They harangued against President Trump’s economic record and called out corruption for what it is — self-promotion over the interest of US citizens.

Georgia’s Senator-elect Jon Ossoff made Trump’s assault on the rule of law practical. In everyday terms he pointed out to voters how democracy and economic distress depend on each other. He argued that “corruption is why nothing works for ordinary people.”

We are ready as a nation to reintroduce the importance of science to accomplish these goals.

In a recent commentary in the journal, Nature, a group of authors argue that “science is an essential backbone of democracy.” Science as a source of discovery, expertise, and innovation is intertwined, they continue, with democracy as a set of institutions, rules, and procedures. They put forward three pillars through which science enables democracy:

Citizens have legitimate concerns, the scientists agree, “about economic restructuring, regional inequities, how migration is governed, technological changes, ageing populations, climate risks and geopolitical instability.” Then again, “expertise strengthens democratic legitimacy when it is transparent, diverse, independent and open to scrutiny — as prescribed by the rules of science.”

Democracy — when it lives up to its responsibility as the rule of law — contributes to a decrease in CO2 emissions. Climate change, like voting in a democracy, is a collective action problem — a conflict between the individual interest and the group interest.

Congress can look ahead and back science-based clean energy in a variety of ways, from policies to investment, all of which will address the affordability crisis, improve public health, and protect the environment, says Gretchen Colman, president of the Union of Concerned Scientists. That will include renewables and clean transportation, modernizing the power grid, protecting our air and water, and more.

Science-based legislating, Colman continues, must be based “on facts and evidence, not political whims. We’ll petition Congress to restore funding for science, protect federal science agencies, and re-establish safeguards for federal scientists to carry out their work and communicate about it free from political interference.”

A national return to scientific reasoning is underway. Gina Hinojosa, elected as the first Texas Democratic governor, has called for an immediate moratorium on data center development. She has accused developers of “raising our utility rates and depleting our water.” As reported on Bloomberg Green, she’s vowed to halt approvals until “a legislative session that puts Texans first so that we don’t have any more communities in Texas who are powerless against these global corporations.”

Indeed, the output of data centers must also be questioned. Few AI-generated findings are peer-reviewed or tested in laboratories. Talk about mixed messages! We return to a nostalgic past by foregrounding large language models that are programmed to present particular, narrow, and self-serving points of view — generally by billionaires seeking to protect their wealth. It’s time to tax these rare individuals accordingly, eliminate any incentives for their built structures, and require them to provide major renewable energy contributions to communities that house them.

Yes, energy plays an increasingly important role in today’s world. Instead of treating electrification as an option to move us toward decarbonization, it’s time to see it is essential — we need to electrify everything quickly. In the US, more than 60 state and local governments are similarly in court to seek damages from oil companies. These are the lawsuits that Democrats in Congress should be pursuing as complements to transitioning virtually all building, transportation, and industrial systems to electric. This means subsidizing heat pumps and EVs and solar panels and home battery storage — subsidies are far cheaper than funding wars for oil.

The ground-breaking election events have hoisted non-traditional candidates to levels of power and influence, as the New York Times chronicles:

Political analyst Chris Armitrage suggests another important positive trend. “The thing that lasts past January 2027 is the voter who had never voted in a midterm, did it in 2026, and now has the habit,” he concludes.

The urgency of climate change has brought democracy to a critical moment in time. Whether longtime, non-traditional, or new voters, we must continue to argue for inclusive and participatory democracy to address climate change. Shaped by democratic governance, a robust civil society, and innovative advocacy strategies, collective climate action can be a force for the right to a healthy planet.

#5854. DUOTAP Trial: Postdilation Sets Up TAVR for Success

— Extra step optimizes transcatheter heart valve geometry for better downstream hemodynamics

Routine postdilation conferred several upsides in transcatheter aortic valve replacement (TAVR) with a balloon-expandable platform, according to emerging trial evidence.

In the DUOTAP trial, routine postdilation largely corrected any Sapien device asymmetry, with transcatheter heart valve (THV) asymmetry index on fluoroscopy reduced by 70% compared to TAVR without postdilation, in the optimization of THV geometry, according to Maximilian Autherith, MD, of the Medical University of Vienna.

This was associated with downstream benefits, such as a 67% reduction in more-than-mild paravalvular leak (PVL) on pre-discharge echocardiography after TAVR, and a 63% reduction in hypo-attenuated leaflet thickening (HALT) per cardiac CT at 3 months, he reported at the Transcatheter Cardiovascular Therapeutics annual conference hosted by the Cardiovascular Research Foundation, with study results simultaneously published in JAMA Cardiology.

"However, what we don't know to date is if this also translates into longer valve durability and ultimately better clinical long-term outcomes. This remains to be investigated," Autherith cautioned during a press conference.

Even so, the "really impressive" lowering of HALT in particular was highlighted by Adnan Chhatriwalla, MD, of Saint Luke's Mid America Heart Institute in Kansas City, Missouri. Considering the lower PVL as well, "these data suggest that we should routinely do [postdilation]," Chhatriwalla commented during the press conference.

In TAVR, postdilation is the controversial step of reinflating the balloon a second time inside the newly deployed THV, giving it a so-called "double tap" that in theory covers the operator's bases for optimizing THV geometry in the absence of a good way to gauge device expansion. If left alone, asymmetric device expansion may be associated with higher residual gradients, PVL, and impaired THV performance.

Safety is a major concern with postdilation, however, especially when it comes to possible annulus rupture, valve migration, and conduction system disruption.

"So you don't want to take it for no reason," said Susheel Kodali, MD, of NYU Langone Health in New York City, another panelist at TCT.

Kodali said that echocardiographic and fluoroscopic characteristics will be key to defining who gets TAVR with postdilation. He also cited predilation and longer balloon inflation as other strategies for optimizing THV valve expansion -- which is really the ultimate goal for the patient.

"It's valve expansion to the maximum that's needed for that patient, and that's where AI [artificial intelligence], echo, fluoro, all of these things are going to help us understand who to do [postdilation] in," Kodali commented. Currently, "we just inflate, deflate, and don't assess how is the valve expanded. I think we need to be better at that."

Reassuringly, DUOTAP reported a favorable safety trend for routine postdilation, albeit without reaching a statistical difference. Procedural or early clinical adverse events at 30 days reached 24.3% with postdilation versus 31.1% with controls. A new conduction system disorder requiring pacemaker implantation was logged in 20.3% and 21.6% of patients, respectively.

"Certainly yes, there may be some consequences to universally doing a double tap. But having said that, we very clearly often will see asymmetric expansion," said Sammy Elmariah, MD, of UCSF Health in San Francisco. "Some individuals don't pursue it and don't postdilate. And so at least this gives us more motivation to at least, in a selective fashion, to start postdilating those valves and to really aim for symmetric expansion of the valve."

The single-blind, single-center trial enrolled people with severe aortic stenosis undergoing transfemoral TAVR with a Sapien balloon-expandable platform. Participants had to have no high-risk features on CT, no bicuspid aortic valves, and no valve-in-valve procedures, among other exclusion criteria.

Investigators randomized 148 TAVR patients 1:1 to postdilation or no postdilation.

Autherith noted that this was a typical TAVR cohort with a median age of 81 years, 47% women, and a EuroScore-II of 2.3%. The THV sizes chosen were a mix of 23 mm (29.0%), 26 mm (48.7%), and 29 mm (22.3%). A Sapien device with Resilia tissue was given to 42.6% of the group.

Fluoroscopic analysis indicated that postdilation corrects THV asymmetry by inducing valve height reduction on the outer curve of the device.

Postdilation consistently corrected asymmetry across prespecified subgroups including those by sex, THV size, and aortic valve calcification. Only on a post hoc exploratory analysis did a signal emerge that possibly people who got predilation did not have the same benefit of postdilation, according to Autherith.

"Regarding the subgroup analysis, these were only 11 patients receiving predilation, so confidence intervals were quite wide. I think it's difficult to interpret, but of course this is also an important consideration," he said during the press conference.

Other findings include postdilation being tied to increased Doppler velocity index and reduced residual trans-prosthetic gradients on echocardiography. Cardiac CTs also linked the intervention to valves with larger inflow and midframe areas, plus an increase in outflow area, at 3 months.

#5853. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5852. Because Women: Rethinking What Freedom of Movement Can Feel Like

Most women know the unspoken checklist before getting into a car. Getting from one place to another is rarely just a matter of distance; it comes with a quiet, constant layer of preparation and mental energy.

At Waymo, we believe women should get that energy back.

Our new film looks at what happens when the stress is taken out of the journey. Waymo allows our riders to take a work call, sit in total silence, or play music as loud as they want. We’re offering a space entirely on our riders’ own terms.

Because having more choice in how we get around matters.
