#5851. Solar carport project at California medical plaza solves tricky energy billing

Watthub Renewables and Sunrock Distributed Generation completed a 322-kW solar photovoltaic system at Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza, the three-story Class A medical office building in Irvine, California. Solar modules were installed to carports covering approximately 108 parking spots, turning bare asphalt into shaded, covered parking and into the building’s primary source of electricity.

Watthub Renewables, Sunrock Distributed Generation and SunRenu Solar completed a 322-kW carport solar project at Irvine Heritage Medical plaza in Irvine, California. Credit: Watthub Renewables

The system is expected to generate approximately 515,690 kWh in its first year, covering the majority of the plaza’s annual electricity consumption. It was delivered under a 25-year power purchase agreement requiring no upfront capital from Irvine Heritage’s ownership. Watthub and Sunrock DG financed, engineered and contracted by SunRenu Solar. Sunrock DG now owns and maintains the array, while the property buys the power it produces at a rate below Southern California Edison’s.

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza was built in 2008 on just over two acres, with approximately 40,000 sq ft of medical suite.

“Medical office is one of the few property types where the parking lot is part of the patient experience,” said Aaron Moston, director at Watthub Renewables. “Somebody’s coming out of a medical or dental procedure at two in the afternoon in August. Whether their car is 140 degrees or 95 degrees is not a small thing. Carports were the right structure here before we ever ran the production model — the fact that they also happen to be the best south-facing real estate on the property is what made the project work.”

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza is master metered, meaning the entire building draws electricity through a single Southern California Edison (SCE) account on the GS-3-TOU commercial rate, and that cost flows through to tenants inside their charges rather than through individual utility bills. For years, that meant every practice in the building carried a share of an electric bill it could not see or control in a utiliyt territory with rate increases and, during the summer, on-peak energy.

Since solar generation is netted at the master meter, the savings are not captured by one tenant — they reduce the building’s total electricity cost.

“A master meter is usually described as a disadvantage — tenants have no visibility into their usage and the owner absorbs every swing in the energy bill,” said Wilson Chang, CEO at Sunrock DG. “For a solar project it’s the opposite. One meter, one interconnection, one bill to offset and every suite in the building benefits automatically. It’s the simplest way to bring solar to a multi-tenant property.”

Earlier this year, developed another 300-kW solar carport project for the Foothill Medical Association in Foothill, California. The system was also commissioned with a PPA.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5850. Physician Groups Give Mixed Reviews on Medicare Fee Schedule Final Rule

— Pay cut, modifier-25 rule are panned, but payment expansion for primary care gets good marks

Physician groups had mixed opinions on the final Medicare Physician Fee Schedule rule for 2027, which included slightly increased pay cuts for doctors who treat Medicare beneficiaries, compared to a proposed rule released in July.

The rule issued Wednesday by CMS includes a 2.31% decrease in the conversion factor -- the multiplier used to determine payment for each service or procedure performed -- for physicians who don't participate in an advanced payment model (APM). There is also a smaller decrease of 1.83% for physicians participating in APMs. The decreases in the proposed rule issued in July were 1.68% and 1.19%, respectively, for the two groups.

"Medicare should reward clinicians for keeping people healthy," HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. said in a press release. "We are putting prevention and primary care at the center of Medicare, closing payment loopholes, and cracking down on waste. This rule directs resources toward coordinated care that puts patients first. We are changing what Medicare pays for to change what our health system delivers."

Not surprisingly, physician groups were unhappy with the pay cut.

"The American Academy of Neurology (AAN) is deeply concerned with year-over-year cuts to physician payments and is committed to working with Congress to avert this cut before it goes into effect for 2027," the organization said in a statement. "The AAN will continue to work with legislators in support of payment reform efforts to promote a sustainable payment system, such as ensuring physicians receive an inflationary adjustment tied to the Medicare Economic Index."

"The continued erosion of Medicare physician payments, combined with policies that fail to recognize the complexity of care provided by rheumatologists, threatens the stability of physician practices and patients' access to essential care," William Harvey, MD, MSc, president of the American College of Rheumatology (ACR), said in a statement. "The ACR urges Congress and CMS to pursue meaningful, long-term payment reforms that account for the real cost of practicing medicine and protect access to specialty care, particularly for patients in rural and underserved communities."

Physician organizations also panned CMS's decision to implement the proposed "modifier-25" rule, which says that if two or more services are performed on the same day, the most expensive service (either surgical or evaluation and management [E/M] visits) would be paid at 100%, and all other surgical procedure(s) or E/M visit(s) would be paid at a 20% discount.

"Congress must intervene to prevent further harm by immediately fixing cuts to the conversion factor, along with changes to modifier 25," Anders Gilberg, senior vice president for government affairs at the Medical Group Management Association, said in a statement.

However, other clinicians -- such as those who work in accountable care organizations (ACOs) -- were pleased with several of the changes. In a statement from Accountable for Health, which represents ACOs, CEO Mara McDermott said her group "welcomes CMS's continued efforts to move Medicare away from a broken fee-for-service system that rewards volume and toward one that holds providers accountable for quality and spending."

The fee schedule "takes several meaningful steps in that direction. It establishes a 32% payment increase for qualifying visits by clinicians in the Medicare Shared Savings Program and LEAD Model [two Medicare ACO models], increases from 50% to 75% the credit ACOs receive for savings achieved in prior years, and allows eligible ACOs to reduce or eliminate Medicare Part B cost-sharing for beneficiaries," McDermott noted.

The American Academy of Family Physicians (AAFP) praised the rule's expansion of the Primary Care Exception, which allows qualifying residents to provide more complex office and outpatient care without a teaching physician in the room. "This change will give residents additional experience caring for complex Medicare patients as they prepare for independent practice," the AAFP said in a statement.

The academy also applauded the rule's expansion of payment for more comprehensive primary care services, including shared medical appointments, maternity care, tobacco cessation, and advance care planning.

The Primary Care Collaborative echoed those comments, noting that the rule also included reimbursement for health coaching and more resources to integrate behavioral healthcare. "These changes -- especially when paired with an effort to more accurately price primary care -- are important steps toward a more effective system focused on better health," President and CEO Ann Greiner said in a statement.

ATA Action, the lobbying affiliate of the American Telemedicine Association, praised CMS for delaying by 2 years a rule requiring that only clinical staff directly employed by the billing practice may furnish remote physiologic or therapeutic monitoring services. "CMS listened," said Kyle Zebley, CEO of the ATA and executive director of ATA Action, in a statement. "By delaying this requirement, the agency has given patients, practices, and health systems time to keep remote monitoring programs running while we work toward a lasting solution."

Even with all the applause, the reimbursement cuts remained top of mind for many groups.

"These cuts are not happening in isolation," Jerry Penso, MD, president and CEO of the American Medical Group Association, said in a statement. "Our members are absorbing reductions to physician and clinician reimbursement and outpatient drug reimbursement at the same time they face a trillion dollars in pending Medicaid cuts. Each one makes it harder to sustain the staff, technology, and care models patients depend on. Congress must enact lasting reforms now, before access to care suffers."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that the payment discount for the second same-day procedure paid under the modifier-25 rule will be 20%.

#5849. Electric Buses Need a Plan for the Next Operating Contract

Greater Manchester is entering a new stage of bus franchising. In September 2026, Go-Ahead announced that Go North West had won two Bee Network operating contracts covering 38 routes and approximately 60 buses, with service beginning in March 2027. Eighteen of those routes will operate from a new depot in Leigh. The award is a reminder that operating companies can change while the depots, charging infrastructure and grid connections supporting the passenger network remain in place for decades.

Electric-bus procurement adds a long-lived physical commitment to contracts that are often much shorter. An operating agreement may run for five to seven years. A depot building, substation or multi-megawatt electricity connection can remain useful through several such contracts. Buses can sometimes be transferred between operators, but land, cable routes, electrical capacity and installed charging equipment are tied to particular sites. Those assets need to support the passenger service even when the company responsible for driving the buses changes.

Consider an authority retendering electric-bus routes. The incumbent has used a convenient depot and financed chargers and a substantial grid connection. A competing operator submits an attractive price and a credible operating plan, but has no assured right to use the depot and electricity infrastructure. It might need to negotiate with the incumbent, find alternative premises or secure another grid connection, potentially adding years of work and substantial costs. A nominally competitive tender can become much less contestable once those obligations are included.

South Yorkshire has taken a deliberate approach to this problem. In March 2026, the South Yorkshire Mayoral Combined Authority announced the purchase of five major depots, adding them to the publicly owned Leger Way depot in Doncaster. First Bus and Stagecoach will lease the acquired premises back during the transition. The authority intends to use the estate for franchised operations and electric-bus infrastructure, with its first franchised services scheduled for September 2027. Six operators reached the next procurement stage for the initial packages at Olive Grove and Leger Way.

This does not establish that every city should own every depot. London has long maintained a publicly specified, competitively operated bus network while many garages remain outside direct Transport for London ownership. Secure leases, access rights, assignable contracts and specialist infrastructure providers may preserve the same continuity, depending on the local market. The practical test is whether a qualified successor operator can obtain the depot, buses, charging capacity and operational information required to take over on the contracted date.

Remaining asset value is central to that test. If an operator finances twenty-year charging infrastructure under a five-year contract, it needs to know what happens to the investment if another operator wins the routes. The authority must also avoid paying for a useful electrical connection and then finding it cannot make that capacity available to the next contractor. Depreciation, compensation, asset transfer, warranties, software access and grid-connection permissions need to be settled before the first contract ends, ideally before it starts.

England’s January 2026 bus-franchising guidance recognizes the importance of depots, charging infrastructure, transfer arrangements and competition in subsequent procurements. The engineering of electric buses has advanced considerably. Public authorities now need procurement structures that keep the fleet and its supporting infrastructure useful as routes, contractors and technologies evolve.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing article examines the second-contract test through Greater Manchester and South Yorkshire, including asset access, residual values and operating continuity. It draws on my open report, The Bus Is Not the System, comparing metropolitan bus governance and electrification across Britain and Ireland.

#5847. Two New Commissioners Headed to the Arizona Corporation Commission

Phoenix — Come January, the Arizona Corporation Commission (ACC) will be bi-partisan as two Democrats, Clara Pratte and Jonathon Hill, have been elected to the ACC. They will be joining current Republican commissioners Lea Márquez Peterson, Rachel Walden, and René Lopez. The election comes as the Commission is considering rate increases for Arizona Public Service Company (APS) and Tucson Electric Power Company (TEP), and mechanisms that would allow both utilities to increase rates every year with less stakeholder input, and as the Commission begins to consider the utilities’ proposed long-term resource plans.

As a recent Sierra Club report makes clear, the ACC wields a tremendous amount of power over the future of Arizona’s energy landscape, public health, and utility bill increases, especially as the state experiences a fossil gas rush to power data centers. Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country and with Arizona’s largest utility, APS, opting to run the dirty Four Corners coal plant for more than another decade. Yet, up until now, the Commission has made a habit of increasing ratepayer bills, opting for expensive fossil fuels and cutting programs that require utilities to utilize solar energy to generate electricity and to conserve energy, lower electric bills, and invest in energy efficiency.

In response, Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr issued the following statement:

“These election results should act as a wakeup call to the rest of the Arizona Corporation Commission. Arizonans are tired of being put on the backburner, bearing the burden for expensive fossil fuels that harm our neighbors and contribute to climate change. We share in the optimism of our fellow voters and will continue to carefully monitor the ACC as our work to promote clean air, clean water, and healthy communities and a healthy climate enters a new chapter.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. #5846. At $11,995, Could This 2013 Cadillac Escalade Hybrid Make You 'Slade In The Shade?

Today's Nice Price or No Dice Cadillac Escalade is a rare Hybrid model with two electric motors sandwiched between its V8 engine and 4WD. Once very expensive, we'll have to see if this Champagne-hued luxury SUV now fits our beer budget.

For the longest time, Bentley Motors and Rolls-Royce were intrinsically intertwined, with Bentley often getting the short shrift in the partnership. In the latter half of the 20th Century, Bentley's cars often felt like an afterthought compared to the Parthenon-grilled Rolls models. That's an unfortunate bit of history, as before Rolls-Royce bought Bentley in 1931, the latter company built entire automobiles, rather than just foisting chassis and drivetrains on commissioned coachbuilders like the former.

The 1997 Bentley Brooklands we looked at on Wednesday attempted to distinguish itself from its near-twin Rolls-Royce Silver Spirit by offering a tauter suspension, toned-down body-color grille surround, and a turbocharger bolted to its 6.75-liter V8 engine. Presented in excellent condition and, in the seller's words, "needing nothing," our Bentley came with an $18,000 asking price. That's about a tenth of what the car cost new, but when it came down to brass tacks, opinions were fairly evenly split on its value. Ultimately, the brave—or foolhardy—among us lost out, as the Bentley bombed in an excruciatingly narrow 52% 'No Dice' loss.

Regardless of outcome, yesterday's Bentley was a textbook example of what the ultimate in luxury travel was intended to be in the later decades of the 20th Century. By comparison, the 2013 Cadillac Escalade Hybrid that comes our way today is a more modern execution of that concept.

The Escalade, of course, is the vehicle that arguably saved Cadillac. Originally a slapped-together version of the GMC Denali SUV, the Escalade proved so successful that GM added additional versions and further differentiation with later iterations. Those included the comically weird ESV version based on the Chevy Avalanche pickup. One of the rarest Escalade models is the initial 4WD Hybrid, produced between 2009 and 2013.

The Hybrid Escalade's party piece is the dual-mode AHS2 (Advanced Hybrid System 2, Electric Boogaloo), which replaces the traditional automatic transmission. Co-developed by GM, Daimler-Chrysler, and BMW, the hybrid drive unit was used in several of GM's SUVs, along with Mercedes and BMW sedans and the Dodge Durango. Dual mode means the truck can be powered by the gasoline engine, one of the 60-kilowatt electric motors, or a combination of both. Another 60kW electric motor serves as a generator for the nickel-metal hydride battery pack. This was technology that all three partners in the development consortium thought would pave the way to greater efficiency, but ultimately proved overly complicated for the task. All three dropped the hybrid drive unit by the end of the 2013 model year.

This Escalade Hybrid is one result of that short-lived partnership and product run. According to the ad, it has done 140,000 miles (132k according to the text, 140k according to the sidebar listing details — we'll go with the larger number) and, aside from some minor wear and tear, presents admirably for those miles.

Naturally, the most expensive component of any hybrid or fully electric vehicle is the battery, and it's reassuring to know the pack in this Escalade was replaced at 90K. That gives it at least another 40K if the current pack has the same lifespan as the first.

On the gas side of things, there's a 6.0-liter LZ1 V8, making 332 horsepower and 367 pound-feet of torque all by its lonesome. Specs indicate that, combined with the electric motors, power jumps to 379 horses.

Per the seller, the HV battery isn't the only new component this truck has seen. It also has new shocks and suspension components, refreshed brake pads, and new tires on its chrome-plated factory alloys. The ad claims no issues with the truck's performance, saying that it has been "extremely well maintained with all the expensive items already taken care of."

Overall, the truck looks as nice as its description lets on. The exterior, appropriately enough in metallic Champagne, is said to only suffer from some minor scuffs on the rear bumper cap. Other than that, it still shines and is matched with clear, unclouded headlamp covers.

The interior looks lived-in but far from abused. The leather on the driver's seat has the patina of many posteriors, but seems fully intact. So too does the leather on the steering wheel, albeit with a glossy sheen expected given the truck's age and miles. The only other really glaring issue is the wear evident on the fan control in the automatic climate control panel. Geez, people, just leave it on auto!

This is a three-row SUV with room for seven, so it should be as versatile as it is efficient. It comes with built-in navigation, a tow package, and, most importantly, a clean title.

When new, this Escalade commanded over $77,000 to drive it off the dealership lot. That was a long time ago, with many miles and a long-expired warranty in the rearview, so naturally it should cost less now. We need to decide whether the seller's set price of $11,995 is low enough.

Since this one comes with the promise that all the expensive work is already done, it may be a safe bet to take ownership. Also, I think the less said about the AI-generated image of the truck in the ad as some sort of weird road boat, the better.

What do you say? Is this rare hybrid Escalade worth $11,995 as it sits? Or is that too much for a truck with an abandoned technology, no matter how fancy it is?

Seattle, Washington, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5845. Faropoint building 1st of 16 New Jersey community solar projects

Faropoint, an industrial real estate manager, has started construction on a 1.6-MW community solar installation on the roof of its building in Fair Lawn, New Jersey. The installation is the first of 16 planned Faropoint community solar projects in the state, which will have a total capacity of more than 10 MW.

Solar contractors install the first solar modules on the first solar project in Faropoint’s 16-project portfolio in New Jersey. Credit: Faropoint

The other 15 projects, totaling approximately 8.5 MW, have been accepted into New Jersey’s Community Solar Energy Program. Nine of them have received a notice to proceed, allowing construction to begin.

“We aim to create positive community impact, increase access to clean energy and become a responsible long-term owner in the markets where we operate,” said Brian Nave, VP of asset management and renewable energy at Faropoint. “As one of the top-five small-bay industrial owners in New Jersey, we believe our scale comes with a responsibility to drive meaningful sustainability initiatives. Utilizing previously unused portions of our buildings allows us to support the communities where we invest.”

The 16 projects are expected to generate approximately 12.7 million kWh of electricity a year, which is about the annual electricity use of more than 2,000 homes.

“Managing solar across a large real estate portfolio takes persistence, coordination, and a lot of work behind the scenes,” said Yaniv Kalish, CEO of SolarKal. “Seeing the first of Faropoint’s New Jersey community solar projects move into construction is a major milestone. And with nine more projects cleared to proceed and a half dozen more behind them, that’s real progress for Faropoint and the communities these projects will serve.”

SolarKal, Faropoint’s independent solar advisor, has advised the firm on dozens of projects across its portfolio, providing financial analysis, competitive procurement, developer evaluation and selection and ongoing project management. SolarKal also helped Faropoint select developers able to use available federal clean energy incentives before they are phased out.

Faropoint and SolarKal are also working on community solar projects in Illinois, New York and Maryland.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5844. Positive Antinuclear Antibody Test? This Tool Helps Show What That Means.

— Dramatically cuts time to definitive diagnosis of autoimmune disease in randomized trial

For patients ultimately determined to have an autoimmune disease following a positive antinuclear antibody (ANA) test, a digital aid made what is often a diagnostic odyssey more like a trip to the corner store, results of a randomized trial indicated.

Called AURA, use of the (relatively) simple tool cut the median time to autoimmune disease diagnosis by nearly 70% -- 43 days from the positive ANA test, versus 138 days with usual care, according to April Barnado, MD, MSCI, of Vanderbilt University in Nashville, Tennessee, and colleagues.

When applied to some 2,000 ANA-positive patients, most of whom were not ultimately diagnosed with an autoimmune disease, the tool had an area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.79 (95% CI 0.73-0.86) for identifying those who did, the researchers reported in NEJM AI.

"By leveraging routinely collected, real-time EHR [electronic health record] data within a robust randomized pragmatic design, this study demonstrates a scalable framework for moving predictive analytics from theoretical validation into operational clinical implementation," Barnado and colleagues wrote. "Although future multicenter trials are required to establish external generalizability across diverse community practices, these findings illustrate that tightly integrated, interpretable structured-data models can meaningfully accelerate clinical care timelines for high-risk patients."

AURA stands for AUtoimmune Risk Assessment and combines a variety of demographic and clinical factors to produce a "risk score" for the likelihood that an ANA-positive patient has an autoimmune condition such as lupus or systemic sclerosis. ANAs are a hallmark of these diseases but very far from specific to it. One review from 2018 indicated that 2.5% of the general population has "significantly elevated" ANAs, with the authors writing that "[m]ost people with a positive ANA count are not diagnosed with autoimmune disease, and the probability of future disease is low."

Still, the presence of ANAs certainly raises the likelihood that autoimmunity is the reason. The clinical challenge is to identify those patients for whom it's worthwhile to pursue the additional rounds of testing and physical exams involved in a rheumatologic workup. Diagnostic odysseys are notoriously common for ANA-related diseases, with their highly variable clinical presentations -- Barnado's group noted that 7 years is the average time from symptom onset to diagnosis for systemic lupus.

In an earlier report, Barnado and colleagues described how they distilled data from about 2,000 ANA-positive individuals into a model that would pinpoint those most likely to have an autoimmune disease, via a numeric risk score. These data were of the kind routinely included in patients' EHRs: absolute ANA titer, numbers of billing codes for symptoms consistent with autoimmune disease, platelet count, and presence of a second autoantibody, along with age and sex. This initial training-and-validation model yielded an AUC of 0.83 for triaging patients into a rheumatologic evaluation.

For the current, prospective study, the researchers applied the model to a different set of ANA-positive patients seen at Vanderbilt, randomizing about 2,400 to either have their data analyzed with the model, with the resulting risk score conveyed to the treating clinicians via notification in Vanderbilt's EHR system or to usual care in which clinicians used only their own judgment.

Roughly 200 patients in each group were excluded after randomization, either because they were already scheduled for rheumatologic exams or because they had already seen a rheumatologist outside the Vanderbilt system. That left 954 assessed with AURA and 1,006 in usual care. Of the former, AURA identified 270 as high risk for autoimmune disease, making them eligible for an expedited rheumatology referral.

As expected, most patients in the final cohort were not ultimately diagnosed with an autoimmune disease: Within 800 days of the first positive ANA test, only about 8% of those evaluated with AURA ended up with such a diagnosis, versus roughly 5% of the usual-care group. This difference made sense, Barnado's group explained, because of the expedited rheumatologic exams available to those identified as high risk with AURA, who would thus be diagnosed sooner. One could also imagine that in the usual-care group, absence of an EHR flag to suggest a rheumatologic referral would mean that some high-risk patients would never get one.

Confining the analysis to those in the highest quartile of risk scores indicated that 20% were eventually diagnosed with an autoimmune disease, and nearly all of them got that diagnosis within 3 months of their first ANA-positive test. Notably, patients in the usual-care group were also scored with AURA but this was not relayed to clinicians; only about 12% of those with high scores received an autoimmune diagnosis, and it took considerably longer for those who did.

The researchers boasted that their tool's simplicity, both in the small number of variables employed and its regression-based statistical machinery, should make it more useful in the clinic than those powered by big AI models. "Unlike black-box deep-learning algorithms that can obscure decision-making," they wrote, "our model allows providers to easily verify the clinical factors driving the risk score."

An important limitation was the single-center data source, both for the model's development and its testing in the current study. Also, the authors acknowledged that it was impossible to distinguish "the independent predictive utility of the clinical prediction model from the effect of the early rheumatology referral invitation sent to providers" -- merely sending all ANA-positive patients for rheumatologic evaluation would likely speed up diagnosis, though costly and inefficient.

#5843. Hyundai Motor Group Outlines Future Mobility Technology Roadmap at FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026

HWASEONG, Korea — Hyundai Motor Group (the Group) is co-hosting the FISITA World Mobility Summit 2026 with the International Federation of Automotive Engineering Societies (FISITA). The two-day summit is taking place Nov. 4–5 at Rolling Hills Hotel in Hwaseong, Korea.

Founded in 1948, FISITA is a global organization that brings together automotive engineering societies and industry experts to promote knowledge exchange and technological innovation in automotive engineering. It holds the World Mobility Summit every two years, bringing together executives and technology leaders from global mobility companies to discuss industry challenges and future technology directions, such as artificial intelligence (AI) and vehicle electrification.

This year’s summit is the first to be held in Korea. Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group, is the first Korean to serve as FISITA President.

“Hyundai Motor Group will continue to strengthen its competitiveness in key future mobility technologies and continue to participate in technology exchange and collaboration across the global automotive industry to contribute to the development of the future mobility sector.” –Chang Hwan Kim, Executive Vice President and Head of the Electrification Energy Solutions Tech Unit at Hyundai Motor Group

On the first day, Manfred Harrer, President and Head of R&D Division at Hyundai Motor Group, delivered a keynote address introducing the Group’s electrification strategy. It encompasses battery electric vehicles (BEVs), hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs), extended-range electric vehicles (EREVs) and fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs). Harrer described the Group’s technology development roadmap for responding flexibly to market changes and customer needs.

Harrer also emphasized strengthening future vehicle development capabilities by combining engineering technology with AI-based digital capabilities and connecting the strengths of different technologies to pursue a sustainable mobility future.

In his opening remarks, Kim said that the automotive industry is undergoing an unprecedented technological transition. He emphasized that diverse technology approaches and stronger global collaboration are important to the advancement of future mobility.

Under the theme “Embracing the Complexities of Mobility,” the summit is addressing key technologies and challenges facing the automotive industry, including software-defined vehicles (SDVs), AI, autonomous driving, batteries and energy infrastructure.

Executives and technology leaders from across the global automotive industry are in attendance, sharing perspectives on key technology challenges and future development directions for the mobility industry.

Keynotes and panel discussions are examining major changes in the automotive industry, including advances in AI and software, the evolution of autonomous driving technology and the diversification of electrification technologies. Participants are also discussing technology directions and ways for the industry to collaborate in response to these shifts.

Major automakers are presenting vehicle and technology exhibits during the summit. The Group is displaying the Genesis GV90 and Hyundai TUCSON, along with a battery pack, hydrogen tank and robotics platform.

#5842. Kimi Antonelli Won The F1 Driver's Championship On A Cracked Oil Sump

Twenty-year-old Kimi Antonelli made history on Sunday becoming the youngest Formula 1 Drivers' Champion in the history of the sport and the first Italian driver to win it since Alberto Ascari in 1953. He clinched it finishing third at last weekend's Mexico Grand Prix, sharing the podium with fellow champs and legends in their own rights, Max Verstappen and Lewis Hamilton, who lifted Antonelli up onto their shoulders for an F1 photo op to end all F1 photo ops.

Mathematically, Antonelli's championship win was almost a foregone conclusion at that point, but his picture-perfect victory almost didn't happen that way because it's now been revealed that his final laps in Mexico City were done in a car that was very much on its last legs. Mercedes F1 deputy team principal Bradley Lord appeared on the High Performance Racing podcast after Sunday's race and said Antonelli won it all on a cracked oil sump.

"We got the car back after the race, took the floor off the bottom — covered in oil," Lord recalled. "Sump cracked, and Kimi said he thinks about four laps from the end, he could start to feel the engine just starting to struggle. So, right on the edge of retirement of that race and yet it held together." For what it's worth, non-catastrophic engine issues were a bit of a theme for Mercedes in Mexico as Antonelli's teammate George Russell, who qualified P2, suffered a power unit "deployment problem" right at the start of the race. He lost multiple places quickly and eventually finished sixth.

Cracked sump or not, it's unlikely Antonelli would've been going ham in those final stages of his race anyway as he apparently struggled to even see through his own tears before crossing the finish line as World Champion, according to Sky Sports. In the end, he finished a little over four seconds away from second-place Hamilton, who was eight-tenths off of race winner Verstappen. The Italian driver picked up 15 points, more than enough to secure the biggest trophy in motorsport earlier in life than anybody else who'd ever done it before.

#5841. FDA Approves Dual-Targeted Therapy for Head and Neck Cancer

— Overall response rate was 42% in a population with limited treatment options

The FDA granted accelerated approval to subcutaneous amivantamab and hyaluronidase (Rybrevant Faspro) for the treatment of adults with recurrent or metastatic head and neck squamous cell carcinoma.

The approved indication is for patients whose disease has progressed on or after platinum-based chemotherapy and immunotherapy, and does not include patients with human papillomavirus-associated oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma, the agency noted.

The approval was based on results from the phase Ib/II OrigAMI-4 trial involving 102 patients with recurrent or metastatic head and neck squamous cell carcinoma. The overall response rate was 42% with subcutaneous amivantamab, including a complete response in 15% of patients, with 56% of responders maintaining their response for 6 months or longer.

Currently approved therapies are linked to overall response rates of up to 20% and poor outcomes, the FDA noted.

"Today's approval represents a meaningful step forward for patients with head and neck squamous cell carcinoma, a disease where treatment options have historically been limited and outcomes remain poor," said Angelo De Claro, MD, director of the FDA's Oncology Center of Excellence. "This therapy offers patients and their physicians a new option with the potential to make a real difference in people's lives."

Amivantamab (Rybrevant) targets both EGFR and MET, two key drivers of tumor progression in head and neck cancer. In 2024, the FDA approved intravenous amivantamab plus combination chemotherapy for the first-line treatment of non-small cell lung cancer in patients whose tumors harbor EGFR exon 20 insertion mutations. Last year, the agency approved subcutaneous amivantamab and hyaluronidase for adults across all indications approved for the intravenous formulation of amivantamab.

In a press release from maker Johnson & Johnson, OrigAMI-4 investigator Barbara Burtness, MD, of the Yale Cancer Center in New Haven, Connecticut, noted that the "findings represent important progress and highlight the potential of dual EGFR- and MET-targeting with amivantamab as a distinct treatment approach in recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer."

The prescribing information includes warnings and precautions for hypersensitivity and administration-related reactions, interstitial lung disease/pneumonitis, dermatologic adverse reactions, ocular toxicity, and embryo-fetal toxicity.

Full approval for this indication may be contingent on results from the ongoing confirmatory phase III OrigAMI-5 study, which is evaluating subcutaneous amivantamab in combination with carboplatin and pembrolizumab (Keytruda) as a first-line treatment for patients with recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer.
